# PrusaSLA roadmap

This is the working todo list for turning this PrusaSlicer fork into an SLA-focused slicer. It's meant to be worked through over time, one todo per session, with OpenCode (or any coding agent) or by hand.

- **Background and rules:** [PLAN.md](PLAN.md). Codebase map (section 1), color palette (2.1), file ownership and hotspot files (3.3), definition of done (3.5).
- **Agent instructions:** [/AGENTS.md](../../AGENTS.md). OpenCode loads this file automatically.
- **Tracking:** this file is the only place progress is recorded.

## How to use this file

1. **Pick a todo** whose `needs` are all ticked. In OpenCode, run `/next-todo` to have it picked for you, or `/do-todo M3.4` to name one.
2. **Skip `[human]` todos.** They need a person, for example to supply sample files, make a judgment call, or run a build that takes hours. Agents stop and report when they reach one.
3. **One todo is one branch and one commit (or a small PR):** branch `sla/<ID>-<slug>`. Tick the box **in the same commit** as the work, and add a short result note after it, for example `→ 4f2a1c9, 312 tests pass`.
4. **If a todo turns out bigger than one session,** don't half-finish it. Split it into sub-todos (`M3.4a`, `M3.4b`) in this file, commit the split, then do the first one.
5. **If you're blocked,** leave the box unticked and add a line `  Blocked: <reason>` under the todo.

Sizes: **S** is under an hour of agent work. **M** is one session. **L** must be split before starting.

Milestones are ordered by value but can overlap. Anything whose `needs` are met can start.

---

- [ ] **M0.13** Benchmark harness that writes a metrics JSON (PLAN A6). · M · needs M0.11a, M0.12
  Done when: two runs on the same commit give identical layer hashes, and `doc/sla-fork/baseline.json` is committed.
  Result: hidden test `[benchmark]` in `tests/sla_print/sla_benchmark_tests.cpp` runs the support tool and a full SLAPrint slice per model and writes a stable-key-order JSON. `SLA_BENCH_DIR` names the corpus folder; a `manifest.yaml` in it decides which models run and the JSON names each of them by `id` and `category` only, never by file name or path; without a manifest the folder is scanned as before. `SLA_BENCH_FILTER` narrows the run to a comma separated list of ids and categories, `SLA_BENCH_OUT` names the file. Per model it records triangle count, support point count, support tree triangles and volume, pad volume, layer count, a FNV-1a `layer_hash` of the sliced layers, islands and islands without a support point, the time per phase and the peak working set. A model that throws records `error` and the run continues, and the start and the end of every model are logged with its timing. `doc/sla-fork/tools/bench_diff.py` keys the models on the id and flags a changed `layer_hash`.
   Progress 2026-09-30: manifest reading, `layer_hash`, `SLA_BENCH_FILTER`, the per model logging and the diff tool's id keying are in. Not built or run in this session, so `doc/sla-fork/baseline.json` is still missing and the todo stays unticked until the maintainer produces it from a run of the built binary.

    Result: six merges' worth of mismatches between a declaration and its use, every one read against the real signature. `CavityDetection.cpp`: the centroid returned an Eigen expression in one arm of a conditional operator (C2446), so it is an `if` returning `Vec2d(sum / total)`; `overlaps()` and `union_ex()` take ExPolygons, so a single hole of a layer is wrapped in `ExPolygons{holes[h]}`, the group region starts as `{holes[group.holes.front()]}` and the rest of the group is walked by index (the old `std::next(begin, end)` passed an iterator as the distance). `HollowingLattice.hpp` includes `admesh/stl.h`: `indexed_triangle_set` is a global-namespace type, the header used it without including it, and both of its declarations were lost, which is what the three errors in the .cpp were. `SupportFacetPaint.cpp` and the facet paint test spell it `Domain::indexed_triangle_set`, which does not exist (only `Domain::indexed_triangle_set_with_color` does). `LayerStats.cpp` and `sla_layer_stats_tests.cpp` read `perimeter_coefficient_n_per_mm2`; the field is `perimeter_coefficient_n_per_mm`, the term being N/mm over a length. `SupportPointGenerator.cpp` passed `p.cast<double()`, an Eigen expression, to `squared_distance_to_indexed_lines`, which deduces one `VectorType` from the point and from the hit point, so the two disagreed (C2672); it binds a `Vec2d` first, as the two calls next to it do. `SLAPrintSteps.cpp`: `facet_paint_of()` is a free function and `m_model_height_levels` is private, so the heights arrive as an argument from the friend that calls it, as `slice_volumes()` takes the slice grid; two `model_object()->name()` calls read the field as a function. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests).

- [x] **M0.14c** The MSVC Release build of `sla/batch-m15` still fails in libslic3r with C2672 from the forwarding `add_pillar` in `SupportTreeBuilder.hpp`: the calls at `SLA/SupportTreeUtils.hpp:666` and `SLA/SupportTreeUtilsLegacy.hpp:228` pass five arguments (endpoint, length, start radius, end radius, `StemGeometry`) and `Pillar` has no constructor that takes one. Fix it, no behaviour change. · S · needs M0.14b
    Result: `Pillar` got the constructor `(endp, h, start_radius, end_radius, stem)`, which stores the stem and tapers the given end radius with `stem.end_radius()` - exactly what the `add_pillar` overload with the same five arguments does by hand, and what `add_pillar(headid, length)` does for a pillar hanging from a head, so the forwarded call now builds the pillar that overload was written to build and the mesh does not change. The overload set is why it failed: the forwarding template `add_pillar(Args&&...)` binds its arguments as references, which is a better match for lvalues than the overload's `const Vec3d&` and by-value parameters, so the five argument call went to the template and from there into a `Pillar` constructor that did not exist. Read over every other call of the builder for the same kind of mismatch, none is broken: `add_pillar(headid, length)` and `add_pillar(pillar)` (a copy) are the two argument and copy forms, `add_pillar_base(pid, height, radius, shape)` matches its declaration, `add_head` and `add_anchor` are called with a `Head` to copy and with the six arguments of the `Head` constructor, `add_junction`, `add_bridge`, `add_crossbridge` and `add_diffbridge` are called with the two, three and four arguments their constructors take, and the `Pedestal` of `add_pillar_base` is built from five. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests).

Fix them, no behaviour change. · S · needs M0.14d
    Result: every error read against the real declaration, and none of the fixes changes what the code does. Three are the same mistake: a type the file only forward declared. `PaintOnSupportsDialog.cpp:228` calls `set_visible()` on the separator the constructor stores, so it includes `App/Yoga/Separator.hpp` (the header forward declares it for the pointer). `RotationDialog.cpp` calls `job_manager()` in five places (lines 114, 212, 222, 357 and 413) and the class was only declared, so its member templates could not be instantiated (C2027); it includes `Biz/Platform/JobManager/JobManager.hpp`, which brings `Job.hpp` and `ProgressTracker` with it. `ThumbnailImageGenerator.cpp:210` hands the camera of the fixture view to `set_fixture_view_camera()`, so it includes `App/Platform/CameraSynchData.hpp`, the definition of a struct every header of the view only forward declares. `SlaSupportPreviewService.hpp:193` is `Slic3r::sla::SupportToolModelMesh` (C2872): the fully qualified name is the one line 214 uses for `SupportToolTree` and the one the preview test uses. The three unqualified uses in the same file's `.cpp` (lines 329, 487 and 526) are qualified the same way, beside the two that already were. `JobManager.hpp:169/170` is the SLA archive import: the job is created with one argument, the paths, while `import_sla_archives_local` took four, a `const std::vector<path>&` and a `SlaArchiveRead` the job cannot pass, so `get_args_tuple` asserted that the arguments it was given plus the progress tracker are the ones the function takes. The job function now takes exactly what its job is created with, a stop token, a progress tracker and the paths, and the job is created with a copy of the paths, so it owns them on its worker thread: the vector they are read from belongs to the caller, which is gone long before the job ends, and a job that is given an lvalue keeps a reference to it. The reader seam is untouched: `read_sla_archives()` still takes the replaceable `SlaArchiveRead` the archive tests drive. `SidebarSlaSummary.cpp:551` wrote into a `const std::ostringstream`. `FileLoadingLogic.cpp:1343` handed the temporary `path.filename().string()` to `fmt::make_format_args`, which takes lvalue references (C2664); the file name is a local now and the call is `fmt::format(fmt::runtime(_u8L(...)), file_name)`, the idiom the rest of the layer uses for a translated format string, the same message and the same translation entry. `DrainHoleSuggestion.cpp:117` named `mesh_to_mesh`, which is declared nowhere: the transform of the instance is `mesh_to_world`, and the inverse transpose of its linear part is what carries the facet normal of the raycast into the frame of the layers, so the sign the hole is cut with is the one the comment already describes. Read over the tests of the touched files: `SlaArchiveImportJobTests` drives `read_sla_archives()` with its own reader and does not reach the job function, `DrainHoleSuggestionTests` has an instance that is scaled by two and moved onto the plate whose normal comes out of the inverse transpose as (0, 0, 1), and `SidebarSlaSummaryTests` expects `5:01` and `36:00` out of the stream that is no longer const. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests).

  - [x] **M1.11c** An estimated print time in the SLA sidebar summary, like Chitubox and Lychee show, and in the archive headers that have a print time field. · M · needs M1.11a
    Result: one function, `Domain::sla_estimate_print_time()` in the new `SLA/PrintTime.{hpp,cpp}`, estimates a printer without a tilt: per layer the exposure (bottom, the linear fade over the transition layers the engine already uses, the M2.14b3 raft interface band, normal), the three `wait_*` light-off delays and the lift and the retract at their speeds, including the `bottom_*` set on the burn-in and the `*_2` second stage; summed over the layers. A term with no speed is 0 instead of a division, so a partial config view is safe. `SLAResultData` grew two fields (the hotspot edit is seven lines): the total in `print_time_s`, empty rather than 0 when there is nothing to estimate, and each layer's own time in `layer_print_times_s`; `merge_slices_and_eval_stats()` fills them. `SidebarSlaSummary` shows it as `Print time` under the layer count, `hh:mm` (hours without a leading zero, minutes always two, seconds dropped) and the en dash where there is no value, next to the existing `format_print_time()` tested with the other formatters. The formula, and the two models it sits next to, are written up in `doc/sla-fork/profiling/print-time.md`. The four non-SL1 writers each summed their own terms and did not agree: pm5/pwmx/pm5s/pm7 charged every layer the normal lift and one delay, goo and ctb charged no separation at all, and none counted the second stage; all five now write the shared estimate, and `pm5.md` and `ctb.md` say so on their print time rows. That changes the value those headers carry, so the two `.ctb` expectations were re-spelt over the new terms and the pm5 HEADER +68 field, which nothing read back before, is checked now. 10 domain cases (the hand-built 3-layer bottom+transition+normal case, the fade ramp, the interface band, the two-stage lift on both the normal and the burn-in values, and the not-an-estimate states) plus the writer and formatter cases. The `.sl1`/`.sl1s` export deliberately keeps the SL1 tilt model, so on a Prusa machine the sidebar figure and the `printTime` in `config.ini` are two estimates of the same print; picking one for both is left open. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests in this session).
  - [x] **M1.11d** The resin each model of a plate cures on its own, the way Chitubox and Lychee show it: a secondary figure in the objects list and a per model table in the sidebar summary. · M · needs M1.11a
    Result: one pure function, `SLA::object_resin_use()` in the new `SLA/ObjectResinUse.{hpp,cpp}`, sums the per object slices the result already has into the resin of every model object, and `merge_slices_and_eval_stats()` fills it: the layer loop that merges the plate now keeps the slices per object beside the merged ones (the same `get_all_polygons()` placement, extracted into `place_polygons()` so the raft outline can be placed the same way), and the raft is sliced on its own once per object on the grid the supports were sliced on, because it is part of the support slices and two models that stand close enough share one. THE RAFT RULE: the raft of the print is counted once and split between the models by FOOTPRINT, the widest layer of their bodies, which is what decides how much raft the generator grows around them; asking each model how much raft it sits on would count a shared raft twice and the models would no longer add up to the print. The bodies are counted where no other body covers the same resin and the support trees where no body stands on them, exactly as the plate totals are counted, so the models add up to the figures above them within rounding. `SLAResultData` grew one field for it (the hotspot edit is six lines). The app side is `App/SlaObjectUseRows.{hpp,cpp}` next to the M1.11b issue rows: the ml and the cost of a row come from the same `ResinEconomics` the plate figure uses, so a row cannot disagree with the figure it breaks down, and the cost carries no currency symbol because no config key says which currency a resin is sold in (`bottle_cost` is a plain money amount, so the plate cost is shown the same way). The sidebar summary shows the rows under a `Per model (N)` toggle, but only with more than one model on the plate, and before the first slice it lists the models of the bed with the en dash the plate figures carry in that state; the objects list, which is a table of icons with no room for a second line, shows the same figures as a tooltip on the row of the model. Both are SLA only, like the whole window. 11 cases in `tests/sla_print/sla_object_resin_use_tests.cpp` (two cubes of different size in the ratio of their volumes, the total equal to the sum, a body standing on another model counted once, the support tree around a body, the raft of a shared pad split by footprint in three ways, the widest layer as the footprint, the entries in any order) and 12 in the app suite for the rows, the text and the en dash.   - [x] **M1.12c** `doc/sla-fork/tools/render_icons.py` only renders with cairosvg, which needs the cairo C library and is not installed on the maintainer's Windows machine. Add an Inkscape 1.4 backend and pick it automatically when cairosvg cannot be imported: find the executable through `INKSCAPE` or `shutil.which("inkscape")`, rasterise each needed size with `inkscape <svg> --export-type=png --export-width=<w> --export-height=<h> --export-filename=<png>` into a temporary folder inside the output tree, build the .ico/.icns/.jpg with Pillow from the same code the cairosvg path uses, add `--backend {auto,cairosvg,inkscape}` and `--dry-run`. · M · needs —
    Result: `--backend auto` (the default) uses cairosvg when it imports and Inkscape otherwise; `--backend inkscape` finds the executable through `$INKSCAPE` (a full path or a bare name) and then `shutil.which("inkscape")`, and when neither resolves it exits 2 naming Settings > Apps > Advanced app settings > App execution aliases > Inkscape, which is what the Microsoft Store build needs. The only thing the two backends do differently is `Rasteriser.png(svg, w, h)`, which returns PNG bytes; the .ico, .icns, .png and .jpg are all still built by the same Pillow functions, so nothing is duplicated. Inkscape has no multi-size export, so it runs once per size, writing into a `.render_icons-*/` folder beside the outputs rather than %TEMP% and deleting it in a `finally`, on success and on failure alike; a run killed outright can only leave a folder the .gitignore now keeps out of the repository. It also has a 120 s per-export timeout, because a hung first run is otherwise indistinguishable from a slow one. `--dry-run` (with `--check` kept as an alias) lists the sources, the files it would write and the backend, and reports a missing backend instead of failing, so the choice is visible before anything is written. Checked with a stub `inkscape` and a stub `$INKSCAPE`, off to one side: the four outputs are written and the scratch folder is gone, and a non-zero exit, an export that writes no file, a timeout and an `INKSCAPE` that names a non-executable are each reported with the exit code and Inkscape's last line. Nothing in `resources/icons/` was rendered and no binary was committed, so M1.12 stays unticked until the tool is run for real.

- [x] **M2.5b** The sign of the normal a drain hole carries. M4.8f left a note that the M2.5 drain hole gizmo may store the normal its raycast returns unnegated, which is the opposite of what the cutter needs, so a hole placed by click would be cut out of the model instead of into it. Establish the convention from the engine and from the legacy `GLGizmoHollow`, fix whichever of the gizmo, the engine and the M4.8f suggestion disagrees with it, keep the 3MF files of upstream PrusaSlicer cutting the same way, and test it. · S · needs M2.5
  Result: the convention is the one the engine cuts with, and the one the legacy `GLGizmoHollow` already used: the normal points INTO the material. `sla::to_mesh` builds the cutter as a closed cylinder that sits on the position and runs along the normal, `sla::transform_drainhole_points` pulls its near cap 1mm back along that same direction so the boolean stays well defined, and `create_exclude_mask` reads the axis as `pos + normal * height`, so all three say the same thing. Of the three producers the M4.8f `suggest_drain_hole()` was already right (it decides by the direction of its search rather than by the winding) and the legacy gizmo was right (it stored `-raycast_normal`); the M2.5 gizmo was the wrong one, so a hole placed by click, or dragged to a new spot, was stored with the sign the cutter cannot use. `SlaHollowGizmo::hit_to_object_pos_normal` now ends in a new pure `drain_hole_pos_normal_in_object_mesh()` in `SlaDrainHolesEditing.{hpp,cpp}`, shared with the editor, which transforms the position, carries the normal over with the same inverse transpose as before (so a rotated or non-uniformly scaled volume is unaffected) and negates it, so the gizmo cannot get the sign wrong a second time; its degenerate-normal fallback of +Z follows the same sign. The hole marker needed nothing: it was already drawn from the position along `+normal` like the cutter, so it now matches what is actually cut. Nothing on disk moved: the 3MF writer and reader are untouched, so files written by upstream PrusaSlicer, which stores the negated normal, still load and cut the same way. One engine test in `tests/sla_print/sla_hollowing_tests.cpp`: a click on the bottom of the 20mm cube comes out of the gizmo's own computation at (12, 9, 0) pointing +Z, that hole goes through `transform_drainhole_points` and `sla::hollow_mesh_and_drill` into the hollowed cube, and a ray straight down its axis out through the floor meets no surface of the drilled shell, where the same ray meets the floor of the undrilled copy of that shell (the control, so an empty mesh cannot pass it). NOT BUILT, a human build and run is needed. To check in a running app later: click a hole on a flat bottom and slice, the hole has to come out on the underside.

Default none, so a raft stays the solid slab of today, bit for bit. · M · needs M2.14b1
      Result: four keys in the Raft group under `raft_edge_taper` (orders 6-9, the six order values below them moved up by four): `raft_infill` (None, Grid, Honeycomb, default None), `raft_infill_spacing` (clear size of one cell, 2.0 mm), `raft_infill_wall` (material between two cells, which is also the width of the rim, 0.4 mm) and `raft_infill_skin` (solid material left under the top face, 0.5 mm). All four are in `raft_shape_settings()`, so they show next to the other raft knobs for every raft type that prints a raft and None hides them. The value travels like the other shared knobs: `raft_infill()` in `SLAPrint.cpp` reads the keys into a `Domain::SLA::RaftInfill` (a config saved before they existed gets the solid raft), `raft_preset_to_pad_values` passes it through (no raft type replaces the pattern, that is a tuning question against Lychee and Chitubox like `SKATE_BRIM_FACTOR`) and `make_pad_cfg` sets `sla::PadConfig::infill`; all four keys invalidate the pad step. In the generator, `cut_infill_cells` pulls the outline of a part in by the infill wall to get the solid rim, `infill_cells` lays a lattice of open cells over what is left of it (squares for Grid, hexagons of a hexagonal lattice for Honeycomb, with the wall between two neighbours so that what is left between the cells is the ribs of the pattern) and clips the cells to the rim; the cells run from the bottom face of the raft up to the top of the skin, which is the raft height minus the skin or, in a raft with a cavity, the floor of the cavity the object sits in, so no cell ever reaches under the object. What is left of the interior around the cells gets the walls and the flat ceiling that closes them, and the bottom face becomes the rim ring plus those ribs, so the walls of the cells stand on its edges: the raft is still one closed solid, with a through hole pattern in the layers of the cells and a solid slab in the layers of the skin. A pattern that would need more than 4096 cells, an interior with no cell in it and a skin that swallows the raft are all printed solid instead, because the alternatives are a hole too small to print and a closed pocket no printer can fill. `tests/sla_print/sla_raft_infill_tests.cpp`: the keys reach the pad config and a config without them gets None; None is the same mesh as the default (same index count, same volume, no hole anywhere in it); Grid and Honeycomb give the same bounding box, a smaller volume and a layer inside the cells with less resin in it and holes in it; in the skin the filled raft has as much material as a solid one and no hole at all. `sla_raft_preset_tests.cpp`, `SlaRaftSettingsTests.cpp` and the invalidation key list extended. NOT BUILT (no build in the session).
  - [x] **M2.14b2c** The filled raft of M2.14b2a is not the mesh it was meant to be: its cells are not closed at the top and a raft with no cavity gets no skin under its top face at all. · S · needs M2.14b2a
      Result: two bugs in the infill path of `Pad.cpp`, both found by `sla_raft_infill_tests.cpp` (the filled raft held more resin than the solid one and its top layer was the solid one with a hole in it). `create_infill_geometry` triangulated the *ribs* at `infill_cells_top_z()`, which is the footprint the skin stands on and not the ceiling of a cell, so every cell was open at the top and the raft was no longer one closed solid: `its_volume` came out larger than the solid slab's and the layers through the cells sliced as the full outline instead of a ring of ribs with the cells in it. The ceiling is now triangulated over the cells, which closes each of them and is at the same time the underside of the skin above; the ribs need no face there, they are the skin. `PadConfig3D::infill_cells_top_z()` returned the *higher* of the skin top and the floor of the cavity (`z > floor_z ? z : floor_z`), so a raft with no cavity (`wall_height_mm` 0, the full plate of today) got 0: the cells ran up to the top face, there was no skin and the object would have rested on the open cells. A raft with a cavity whose floor is lower than the skin had its cells cut through the floor the object sits on. It is now `-std::max(infill.skin_mm, wing_height)`, the lower of the two, which is what its own comment has always claimed. Nothing else of M2.14b2a, M2.14b2b or M2.25 moves, so a raft with no infill is still bit for bit the raft of today. `sla_raft_infill_tests.cpp` gained the other half of the bound: a raft with a 1.5 mm cavity, where a filled raft is the solid raft between the top face and the floor of the cavity (the same ring of material with the hole the cavity is) and the pattern is open over the whole height of the slab below that floor. NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests in this session).
  - [x] **M2.14b3** A separate raft interface layer: the skin between the raft and the object, printable with its own exposure and its own thickness, which today is not a layer we can address on its own. · M · needs M2.14b1
    Result: two keys in the Raft group under `raft_infill_skin` (orders 10-11, the six order values below them moved up by two), both in `raft_shape_settings()` so they show next to the other raft knobs for every raft type that prints one and None hides them: `raft_interface_thickness` ("Raft interface thickness", mm, 0-30, default 0) and `raft_interface_exposure` ("Raft interface exposure", s, 0-120, default 0). The band is decided once in the domain beside `sla_bottom_layer_count()`: `Domain::raft_interface_band(raft height, thickness, layer height)` and `Domain::sla_raft_interface(cfg, layer count)`, where the raft height is `pad_wall_height + pad_wall_thickness`, which is `PadConfig::full_height()`. The band is the top that many millimetres of the raft as whole layers; a thickness thinner than half a layer still gets the one layer it rounds to, and a raft that ends on a layer boundary is not one layer taller for it. It never reaches into the bottom layers: a band that would is cut back to them, because a layer that is both is exposed at the bottom exposure - the burn-in is what holds the raft to the build plate. Every writer that stores an exposure per layer applies the band: pm5/pwmx in the LAYERDEF entries, goo and ctb in their layer records, and all three count it in the print time they write (`RaftInterface::print_time_delta_s`); the ctb header's transition layer count grows by the band, the reading of that field that fits both the burn-in and the interface. The .sl1 and .sl1s formats hold a single exposure for the whole print, so the interface exposure has nowhere to go there, `SL1.cpp` says so at the spot, and both tooltips say so for the user. Both defaults are 0, so a print without the interface writes the file of today byte for byte. The interface changes no geometry, so both keys invalidate the export data alone (`slapsMergeSlicesAndEval`). Tests: `SlaLayerHeightTests.cpp` for the band, its layer range, the bottom-layer precedence and the print-time delta; `PM5ExportTests.cpp`, `GooExportTests.cpp` and `CtbExportTests.cpp` slice a cube with a 2 mm raft and an interface of 0.5 mm, read the layer records back and check 35 s on the 11 bottom layers, 6 s up to layer 30, 12 s on the 10 interface layers and 6 s after (ctb also checks that the transition count grows to 20 and that its print time counts the band). NOT BUILT (no build in the session).

  - [x] **M2.16b** The per point tip and stem geometry has to reach the mesh, not only the support point: the tip shape builds a cone or a ball contact, a knot ball is put at the junction, and the stem becomes a polygon prism or tapers, in the default tree and where the branching tree builds the same parts. · M · needs M2.16
    Result: the builder records carry the geometry of their support point: a new `sla::StemGeometry` (cross section 0 or 3 to 12 sides, taper in mm over the pillar, the radius it may not go below) on `Head` and `Pillar`, and `tip_shape` plus `knot_radius_mm` on `Head`. `sla::stem_geometry()` reads them off a support point (a side count outside 3 to 12 stays round, a missing or negative taper is no taper, the minimum radius is `head_fallback_radius_mm`). `pinhead()` got a shape argument: Default builds the two sphere pinhead bit for bit, Cone closes the circle the robe ends at to a point where the front sphere's pole was, Ball builds the whole sphere of the front radius; all three are as long as the default one and reach as deep, so the junction and with it the pillar do not move. `get_mesh(Head)` adds the knot as a ball at the junction. `get_mesh(Pillar)` builds a `polygon_cone` for a stem with sides (4 * sides triangles instead of 4 * steps) and a tapered pillar is a frustum down to `StemGeometry::end_radius()`, which `add_pillar` computes while it builds the pillar and clamps at the minimum radius; `add_pillar_base` ends the base where the pillar is instead of where it starts. Both trees pass it on: the default tree through the head and `create_ground_pillar`, the branching tree through `calculate_pinhead_placement` and `GroundConnection::stem`. The collision checks measure what is there: the new `Head::junction_radius()` is the knot or the back of the pinhead and is used by the ground scan of the default tree, by its ground route and by the leaves of the branching tree; all three tip shapes sink into the model equally deep, so the pinhead clearance estimate does not change. The pad is sampled from the support mesh, so it follows the pillar, and the live preview hash mixes the four fields too, since they change the geometry the preview draws. Tests: `tests/sla_print/sla_support_point_geometry_tests.cpp` (11 cases): the default tip shape builds the pinhead of before bit for bit, a cone tip is smaller and a ball tip bigger than the default one at the same length and depth, the knot grows the head by its ball at the junction, a hexagon stem carries the hexagon of a round pillar of the same circumscribed radius in 24 triangles, the taper thins the pillar towards its base and stops at the minimum radius, a point that asks for nothing keeps the old mesh, and both trees carry the point's shape, knot, sides and taper into the head, the pillar and the base. NOT BUILT (no build in this session). Still open: no UI control for the four fields and no global key.

    Result: By OpenCode, reviewed; two follow-ups fixed a block placed outside its function and a pointer used as an iterator. Twelve `support_preset_<light|medium|heavy>_<head_diameter|pillar_diameter|base_diameter|base_height>` settings in Supports & raft (defaults = the old constants, each with an empty engine invalidation entry); the tool reads them from the selected print preset.
  - [x] **M2.18c** The preset sizes have to reach the generated support tree, not only the point they are stored on: a Heavy point has to get a thicker pillar and a base of its own. · M · needs M2.18b
    Result: by OpenCode; audit: the tip diameter reaches both trees (per point head_front_radius), the pillar diameter and the base size reach only the default tree (the branching tree takes both from the global config), and the penetration is per point (contact_depth) but no preset writes it. New sla::head_back_radius() gives a head the pillar diameter of its own point, clamped into [head_fallback_radius_mm, 4 * head_back_radius_mm]; both trees use it, the pinhead collision distance uses the point's own tip radius instead of the global one, and a thin preset pillar gets the base it asked for instead of being treated as a mini pillar. The live preview hash mixes the per point sizes too. Test: two points with 0.8 mm and 1.8 mm pillars get 0.4 mm and 0.9 mm pillars with their own bases, in both trees. Left: the base size in the branching tree, a penetration value per preset, and the widening of a long thin pillar back to the configured radius.
  - [x] **M2.18d** The M2.18c leftovers: the base size reaches the ground base of the branching tree too, a point's own penetration is used where its head is placed (not only for the collision distance), and a long thin pillar is not widened back to the globally configured radius. · M · needs M2.18c
    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT. New `sla::head_penetration()` and `sla::base_size()` read the per point contact depth and base off a support point; `calculate_pinhead_placement` and `DefaultSupportTree::add_pinheads` build the head with the point's penetration, so the junction (and with it the pillar) follows it. `deepsearch_ground_connection` takes the point the connection starts from and sizes the pedestal from it, and `GroundConnection::full_radius` carries the radius that counts as a full pillar, so `build_ground_connection` adds the base of a point whose pillar is thinner than the configured one. The branching builder remembers which support point every leaf came from (`leaf_pts`) and gives its ground connection the point's own base and radius, which also keeps a long thin pillar from being widened to the node it continues to; the default tree's mini pillar path now widens to the point's own radius (`search_widening_path` returns the radius it was asked for instead of the global one) and does not run at all for a pillar that is already the width its point asked for. Points without a per point size are unchanged. Tests: the branching tree gives the Light and Heavy points their own 2 mm and 4 mm bases, a point without a preset keeps the global one, a deeper contact depth puts the head's junction closer to the model in both trees, and a 12 mm gap under a Light point leaves its pillar 0.4 mm wide in both trees.

- [x] **M2.21** Supports stay on the model after leaving the support tool (user, 2026-09-29), and show in Prepare for every supported object. · M · needs M2.20
  Result: `SlaSupportPreviewService` owns the support tree and raft preview in Prepare: it watches the slicing input, project, build plate and selection, rebuilds changed objects on a worker thread through `SLASupportTool` (never slicing), and lifts the model node to the support elevation via `PlaterScenePresenter::set_sla_lift`. The gizmo no longer draws its own tree. Tested: both suites green 2026-09-30. Awaiting the user's check in the running app.
  - [x] **M2.21b** The support preview keeps up on the maintainer's benchmark figures (50-100 MB): a burst of edits (dragging a point, a slider moving a per point value) ends in one build of the final state, a key that changes while a build runs stops it, and a result of an older key is thrown away. · M · needs M2.21
    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). The new `SlaSupportPreviewSchedule` (`SlaSupportPreviewSchedule.{hpp,cpp}`) owns the debounce and the cancellation per object, behind a two-method timer interface (`ISlaSupportPreviewTimer`, the platform one is `Biz::Platform::TimerQueue`, a test fires it by hand): every key change replaces the build waiting for the object's window (`debounce`, 200 ms, a named constant), a build already running for that object is stopped through the stop `sla::build_support_tree_for_tool` already takes, and `is_current()` compares the request's signature with the object's newest one, so a stale result is never installed. The service keeps one `Pending` per object (matrix, points, configs, elevation, bed transform) and clones the model mesh only when a build really starts, so a burst of edits of a big figure costs one clone and one tree instead of one per event; `start_next_job()` drops the queued jobs whose signature is already stale. The result still comes back through the main thread dispatcher, the main thread never waits for a build, and `build_nodes` still frees the previous tree and raft before installing the new ones, so an object never holds more than the tree it shows. Tests: `test/Slic3r/App/Plater/SlaSupportPreviewScheduleTests.cpp` with a fake timer and a fake builder: four rapid edits build once and for the last signature, every object debounces on its own, a newer edit stops the build in flight and its result is stale, another object's build keeps running, a forgotten object and a project change drop everything, and a debounce of a gone service builds nothing. Nothing slices: the meshes still come from `SLASupportTool` alone. Known gap: the mesh clone of a build is still taken on the main thread, now once per burst instead of once per event.
  - [x] **M2.21c** Starting a support preview build no longer stalls the main thread on a 50-100 MB figure: the main thread hands the worker a snapshot of the model's meshes instead of a copy of the model, and the merging, the AABB and the tree are built on the worker. · M · needs M2.21b
    Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). Option A, because the model already stores its geometry as immutable shared data: a `ModelVolume` holds its mesh as a `std::shared_ptr<const TriangleMesh>` and every copy of a volume shares that pointer, so the clone of M2.21b never copied a vertex either, it copied the settings, facets, points and instances around the mesh, O(model) on the main thread for every build. The new `sla::support_tool_model_mesh` is the snapshot: the MODEL PART volumes as `{shared_ptr<const TriangleMesh>, Transform3d}` parts, one shared pointer and one matrix each, no vertex and no config. `build_support_tree_for_tool` and `generate_support_points_for_tool` got an overload over that snapshot, which `build_object_mesh` (the merge, the stats), `AABBMesh`, the tree and the pad all run in, and the `ModelObject` ones now delegate to it, so the support tool gizmo is untouched. `SlaSupportPreviewService::start_due_build` takes the snapshot instead of cloning and the worker builds from it, which is the whole of the change on that side. No data race: the meshes behind the pointers are const and the model replaces them (`set_mesh`), it never rewrites one in place, which is the same guarantee the clone gave (the one in-place writer, `scale_geometry_after_creation`, is documented to run only while the meshes are not shared yet, at load time); the volume matrices and the support points are copied on the main thread as before. M2.21b is untouched: the debounce, the cancel through the worker's stop, `is_current()` and the result through the main thread dispatcher all stand, and nothing slices. Tests: `tests/sla_print/sla_support_tool_tests.cpp` (the snapshot is the volume's own mesh down to the vertex buffer and leaves out what is not a model part, a snapshot keeps the geometry the model had after its mesh and its placement changed, the tree, the raft and the generated points out of a snapshot are the ones out of the object) and two cases in `test/Slic3r/App/Plater/SlaSupportPreviewScheduleTests.cpp` with a fake builder: a burst of edits of a small model and of a 300 x 300 quad one builds once each and copies zero vertices (counted against the model's own buffers, so the check does not depend on the mesh size), and a build that started keeps the mesh it was started from while the model swaps that volume's mesh and moves it. Not checked in a running app.

- [x] **M2.26** A per-point "may rest on the model" switch on the support point ([supports/lychee-parity.md](supports/lychee-parity.md) gap 8 / row 11): Lychee lets the user mark a single support as allowed (or forbidden) to start on the model, while `support_buildplate_only` and `support_max_weight_on_model` only do it for a whole object. A tri-state on the point (Inherit / Allow / Forbid) picks the route of the pillar where it would end on the model, both in the support tool and in the sliced print. · M · needs M2.16c
  Result: `SupportPoint` carries a tri-state `on_model` (Inherit / Allow / Forbid, default Inherit = the object's `support_buildplate_only`, so today's trees are the trees of today). It is in `SupportPoint::operator==` and in the preview key, and saved in the 3MF as `om`: a project without the key reads back as Inherit, which is what such a point did anyway. The engine asks one question, `sla::may_rest_on_model(sm, sp)` in `SupportTreeUtils.hpp`, and `any_may_rest_on_model(sm)` for the whole object: the default tree keeps a Forbid head off the model (it is routed to the plate in `routing_to_model`, and dropped when there is no route, the fallback the global switch has always used), keeps an Allow head on a plate-only object (the model-facing step is only skipped when no point asks for a model anchor), and the branching tree reads the state of the leaf in `add_mesh_bridge` and samples the model surface when any point allows one. The support tool gets a "Support on model" dropdown next to the M2.16c per-point geometry, written on the selection with the SLA undo snapshot and blank ("Mixed") for a selection that disagrees. Tests: `tests/sla_print/sla_support_on_model_tests.cpp` puts a point over a ledge (a plate route exists) and into a pocket (none does, so the model is the only place its support can end), for both trees; `test/Slic3r/App/Plater/SlaSupportOnModelTests.cpp` next to `SlaSupportGeometryTests.cpp` covers the editing, the selection value and the preview key, and the 3MF round trip in `3MFTests.cpp` got the new key. `supports/lychee-parity.md` row 11 is covered and gap 8 closed.     Result: by OpenCode, NOT BUILT (a human builds and runs the tests). Three object level keys next to the hollowing ones, all in the `Print_Hollowing_Hollowing` group, all with a tooltip and all at the value that keeps today's print: `hollowing_infill` (a combobox None / Grid / Cubic over the new `sla::HollowingInfillType`), `hollowing_infill_spacing` (mm, 0.5 to 30, 3) and `hollowing_infill_strut` (mm, 0.1 to 10, 0.5), each with an invalidation entry that re-runs `slaposHollowing`. `make_hollowing_infill_cfg` (SLAPrint.cpp) reads them into the new `sla::HollowingInfillConfig` the way `raft_infill` reads the raft ones, so a config without the keys is the plain cavity. The new `SLA/HollowingLattice.{hpp,cpp}` is the geometry: `hollowing_lattice_axes` puts one axis per strut spacing, symmetric about the middle of the interval and as many as fit (at most 128, the spacing stretched to spread them when the cap bites), `make_hollowing_lattice` builds square prisms of the strut width over the bounding box of the interior and returns **one mesh per strut direction** (a Grid is the columns of a square grid along Z, a Cubic those plus the same columns along X and along Y), and `subtract_lattice_from_cavity` cuts them out with `MeshBoolean::cgal::minus`, one direction at a time. A strut is never wider than nine tenths of the spacing, so the struts of one direction never touch and a bundle is a set of disjoint closed solids, which is what a CGAL boolean takes (it throws on a self intersecting input); the directions cross each other, which is why they are three booleans and not one. The shape of the change is that the struts are cut out of the *interior* and not unioned into the model: `hollow_model` calls `subtract_lattice_from_cavity` after the lossless simplification of the interior, so the one CSG difference of the hollowing step is the cavity without the struts. Everything downstream then needs no new code: the struts are printed as solid material, the 2D `slice_csgmesh_ex` of the slice step cuts them out of the layers with the same `diff_ex` it already used, the fallback path and `create_exclude_mask` see an interior like any other, and the drain holes are cut at the later `slaposDrillHoles` step, which opens a strut standing across a hole as well, so a strut cannot plug one. None skips all of it and today's hollow is today's hollow bit for bit. Tests: the new `tests/sla_print/sla_hollowing_lattice_tests.cpp` (the three keys in the hollowing group with their defaults, `make_hollowing_infill_cfg` with and without the keys, the axes and the pattern of a Grid and a Cubic, None leaving the cavity untouched, a grid adding resin with the shell still a closed solid and every strut at least 0.2mm inside the outer surface, and a drain hole through a column of the grid leaving the struts and the floor open) plus the three keys in the invalidation test. Known gaps: no UI control yet, that is M2.29b; the lattice is axis aligned, so a strut in a part that is not axis aligned is trimmed to the cavity by the boolean rather than following the part; and a strut wider than the spacing is silently narrowed to nine tenths of it.

## M3: Resin profile import (Chitubox, Lychee and others)

**Goal:** people coming from Chitubox or Lychee can bring their resin profiles with them. They drop in the profile file, check a mapping report, and save it as a PrusaSLA material preset.

### Design

**Why a mapping report is needed**

Other slicers and PrusaSLA don't describe a resin the same way:
- Chitubox and Lychee target Z-lift MSLA printers: lift height and speed, retract, rest times, LED PWM, and a fixed-count block of bottom layers.
- Prusa SL1/SL1S use a **tilt** mechanism: `tilt_*`, `tower_*`, and settings that differ above and below `area_fill`. They also use `faded_layers`, which steps exposure down gradually (between 3 and 20 layers) instead of switching abruptly after the bottom layers.

Some values therefore map exactly, some need a unit conversion, some can only be approximated, and some can't be carried over. The importer **never loses a value silently.** Every source key ends up in the report with one of these statuses:

| Status | Meaning | UI color token |
|---|---|---|
| Exact | Copied as-is | `AccentPrimary` |
| Converted | Unit or shape changed, for example mm/min → mm/s, or per-litre price → bottle cost | `AccentSecondary` |
| Approximated | Semantics differ; a closest value was chosen | `Warning` |
| Not applicable | Doesn't apply to the target printer, for example lift settings on a tilt printer | `Text` (disabled) |
| Unknown | Key not recognized; kept only in the report | `Text` (disabled) |

**Pipeline**

```
file ──► sniff() picks a reader ──► IResinProfileReader::read()
     ──► ForeignResinProfile[]        (neutral, source-agnostic; a file may hold several)
     ──► ResinProfileMapper::map(profile, target printer, base material)
     ──► MappingResult { PresetValueMap values; vector<MappingNote> notes; conditions }
     ──► review dialog (GUI) or JSON report (CLI)
     ──► PresetInteractor::save_user_preset(...)   (user sla_material preset that inherits from the base)
```

**Code locations** (in `src/slic3r-shared/`, following the `Biz → Domain` layering)
- `include/Slic3r/Biz/Preset/Import/ForeignResinProfile.hpp`: the neutral struct. Optional fields for name, vendor, source app and version, and layer height. Bottom layer count and transition layer count. Normal and bottom versions of exposure, lift, retract, rest times and PWM. Density, price and currency unit. Printer hints: resolution, build volume, printer name. A `raw` map with every key as read.
- `.../Import/IResinProfileReader.hpp` with `ChituboxCfgReader`, `ChituboxCfgxReader`, `LycheeLyrReader`, and `SlicedArchiveResinReader` (fallback).
- `.../Import/ResinProfileMapper.hpp`: pure functions with no UI or IO. Most of the tests live here.
- `.../Import/ResinProfileImportInteractor.hpp`: ties reading, mapping and saving together, handles name collisions (`NameValidator`), and does batch import.
- App side: `App/ResinImportDialog.*`, an entry point in `MaterialSelectionDialog`, and drag-and-drop through `FileLoadingLogic`.
- CLI: `slic3r-app-cli --import-resin-profile <file> --printer <printer-id> [--base-material <id>] [--dry-run] [--report out.json]`.

**Known source formats**

| Source | File | What's known | Plan |
|---|---|---|---|
| Chitubox (v1.x) | `.cfg` | Plain text, one `key:value` per line. String values are quoted with `\n` escapes (e.g. G-code), so **split on the first colon only.** Key spellings vary by version (`bottomLayCount` / `bottomLayerCount`, `bottomLayExposureTime` / `bottomLayerExposureTime`). Newer files add two-stage lift keys (`*2`) and `resetTimeBeforeLift`/`resetTimeAfterLift`. One file mixes machine and resin settings. | Reader in M3.4 |
| Chitubox (v2) | `.cfgx` | Structure isn't publicly documented. | Inspect samples first (M3.1) |
| Lychee Slicer | `.lyr` (resin), `.lyp` (printer) | Structure isn't publicly documented. | Inspect samples first (M3.1) |
| Any slicer's sliced output | `.sl1`/`.sl1s` `config.ini` (`expTime`, `expTimeFirst`, `numFade`, …), plus other archive readers from M5 | Exposure and motion settings are embedded in the sliced file | Fallback reader M3.8. Lychee users can export one of these. |
| Resin vendor datasheet | none (typed in by hand) | Around 6 numbers: layer height, exposure, bottom exposure, bottom layers, lift height, lift speed | Quick-entry form M3.11 |

**Chitubox `.cfg` → PrusaSLA mapping**

Unit and meaning questions marked "verify" get settled in M3.1 by comparing a sample file against the Chitubox UI. Names of new keys follow M0.4.

| Chitubox key(s) | Neutral field | PrusaSLA target | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| `normalExposureTime` | `exposure` | `exposure_time` | Exact |
| `bottomLayerExposureTime` \| `bottomLayExposureTime` | `bottom_exposure` | `initial_exposure_time` | Exact |
| `bottomLayerCount` \| `bottomLayCount` | `bottom_layers` | tilt printers: `resin_faded_layers`, clamped to 3–20 (the print preset's `faded_layers` only applies when the resin has none). Generic MSLA: `bottom_layer_count` | Approximated (tilt) / Exact (generic) |
| `transitionLayers`, `fadedLayers` | `transition_layers` | `resin_faded_layers`, both printer classes | Exact |
| `layerHeight` | `layer_height` | `resin_layer_height` (a resin setting since M0.4, not a preset variant condition) | Exact |
| `resinDensity` | `density` | `material_density` | Exact |
| `resinPrice` + `resinUnit` | `price_per_litre` | `bottle_cost = price × bottle_volume / 1000`, only when the unit is per litre. Currency isn't converted, which is noted. | Converted |
| `lightOffTime`, `bottomLightOffTime` | `wait_before_exposure` | `delay_before_exposure: [v, v]` (same value above and below area fill). Verify meaning. | Approximated |
| `resetTimeBeforeLift`, `resetTimeAfterLift` | `wait_before_lift`, `wait_after_lift` | tilt: `delay_after_exposure: [v, v]` for before-lift, drop after-lift. Generic: `wait_*` keys. | Approximated / Exact |
| `normalLayerLiftHeight`, `bottomLayerLiftHeight`, `*2` | `lift_height[_2]` | generic: `lift_height[_2]` and `bottom_` variants. Tilt: not applicable. | Exact / Not applicable |
| `normalLayerLiftSpeed`, `bottomLayerLiftSpeed`, `normalDropSpeed`, `*2` | `lift_speed`, `retract_speed` | generic: `lift_speed`/`retract_speed` in mm/s (source assumed mm/min; verify). Tilt: not applicable. | Converted / Not applicable |
| `normalLightIntensityPWM`, `bottomLightIntensityPWM` | `pwm` | generic: `light_pwm`. Tilt: not applicable. | Exact / Not applicable |
| `bAntiAliasing`, `antiAliasLevel`, `bImageBlur`, `minGreyLevel`, `maxGreyLevel` | raw only | not material settings; shown for information | Not applicable |
| `resolutionX/Y`, `machineWidth/Depth/Height`, `projectType`, `machineType`, `currProfile` | `printer_hints`, `name` | used to **suggest** a matching printer and a preset name; never written to the material | Converted |
| `startGcode`, `layerGcode`, `endGcode`, `displayCorrect*`, `buildAreaOffset*` | raw only | never imported | Not applicable |
| anything else | raw | report only | Unknown |

**Rules**
- **Untrusted input:** cap file size (1 MB for text; for zip-based formats, cap total uncompressed size and entry count), never evaluate G-code, handle a UTF-8 BOM, CRLF line endings, missing keys, and non-numeric values without crashing.
- **Fixtures:** write test fixtures from scratch, with key names taken from observed files. **Don't commit third-party profile files** (vendor profiles are the vendor's content). Real samples go in `local-samples/`, which is gitignored.
- **Interoperability only:** if `.cfgx` or `.lyr` turn out to be encrypted or obfuscated, **don't break the encryption.** Record that in `doc/sla-fork/formats/`, and point users to the sliced-archive fallback (M3.8) and quick entry (M3.11).
- **Traceability:** imported presets inherit from a chosen system base material for the target printer, so tilt, area-fill and other printer-specific settings stay sensible. Only the mapped values are overridden. The origin (source app, file name, import date) goes in `material_source_note`.

### Todos

  Result: `ResinProfileImportInteractor` (Biz layer) runs the whole flow per file: registry reads it (size

  printer (a user or runtime preset would give the new one nothing to inherit), the mapper fills the
  material values plus `material_source_note` = `<source app> <file name>, imported <date>`, and the
  preset is saved through `PresetInteractor::save_selected_preset_as()`, the new non-interactive half of

  of it, save. So the user preset inherits the base (`inherits` = its id) and carries only the imported
  values, and it reloads with the bundle like any preset saved from the material settings dialog. The
  name comes from the profile, sanitized (it becomes a file name, so `:`/`/` and the other illegal
  characters become `_`) and made unique through `NameValidator`, because saving under a taken name
  would *replace* that preset instead of adding one. `import_folder` reads every regular file sorted by
  name, one `ResinImportResult` per file, a file that fails only fills in its own result, and the file
  count is capped. A dry run reports the name it would use and writes nothing. The printer class is
  decided from the base resin (a preset that turns `use_tilt` off lifts the plate) and otherwise from
  `printer_model`, because a `ConfigView` always carries `use_tilt` with its tilt-on default and
  `printer_class_from_config()` would therefore call every printer a tilt printer; worth fixing there.

  - [x] **M3.8b** Read a sliced archive lazily, so a job of many layers can be imported: a reader declares that it opens a container and extracts only the entries it needs, and the 8 MB ceiling applies to what is read into memory instead of to the file. · S · needs M3.8
    Result: `IResinProfileReader` gained `reads_container_lazily()` and `sniff_path()`, both defaulting to false, and `ResinProfileReaderRegistry::read_file()` asks those readers first, on the path alone, and only then applies `MAX_FILE_SIZE` to a plain text file. `SlicedArchiveResinReader` claims a `.sl1`/`.sl1s` by extension plus a zip that opens (opening one reads the central directory alone, so it costs the same on a 300 MB file), extracts only `config.ini` and `prusaslicer.ini` and never a layer image, and caps what it reads against what the central directory declares: `MAX_INI_ENTRY_SIZE` (1 MB) per ini entry, checked by `read_zip_entry()` before a buffer is allocated, and `MAX_ENTRIES_SCANNED` (16384) entries walked through. `.cfg` keeps the 8 MB ceiling. 4 tests in `SlicedArchiveResinReaderTests.cpp` (10 -> 14 cases in that file): a 20 MB archive of a stored padding entry plus a `config.ini` imports through the registry, an ini entry one byte over the cap is refused with the entry name and both sizes in the error, a zip written byte by byte whose `config.ini` claims 3.75 GB is refused instead of allocated, and an archive over the entry cap is refused by name. The user guide's 8 MB paragraph for archives was corrected.
    Left: miniz reads the whole central directory into memory when a zip is opened, so the number of entries an archive may declare is bounded by what that costs before the reader's own cap applies; `bottom_layer_count` is still usually absent because the SL1 writers do not record it (M3.8).

The keys are documented in the new `doc/sla-fork/formats/sl1.md` (the format notes had no `.sl1` one), which records the entry list and the whole `config.ini` key set as unverified on hardware: no file sliced by Prusa's own slicer has been compared and no printer has printed one of these from this fork. - [x] **M3.8d** Opening a zip through miniz reads the whole central directory into memory, so how many entries an archive may declare is bounded by what that costs before the reader's own `MAX_ENTRIES_SCANNED` cap applies: cap what the central directory may cost (or count the entries from the end-of-central-directory record before parsing it)   and refuse an archive over the cap. · M · needs M3.8b
  Result: the counts are taken from the end-of-central-directory record before the open, which is
  the number miniz sizes its directory and its index from. `ArchiveIni.{hpp,cpp}` gained
  `ZipLimits` (max entries, max directory bytes), `ZipDirectoryInfo`, the pure
  `read_zip_directory_info(tail, file_size)` and `is_zip_file(fname)`, and `ZipReader` takes limits
  and carries the reason in `error()`; with no limits (the default, so `SL1Import` is untouched) it
  does not even read the file. The record is looked for from the end of the last 64 kB + 22 bytes
  backwards and only taken when its comment length reaches the end of the file, so a `PK\x05\x06`
  inside a comment is not mistaken for it, and a zip64 record is followed through its locator when
  the 16 bit count or the 32 bit sizes are saturated - the only way an archive can declare more than
  miniz reads. `SlicedArchiveResinReader` passes `MAX_ENTRIES_SCANNED` and the new
  `MAX_CENTRAL_DIRECTORY_SIZE` (4 MB, which 16384 entries of a job stay well under), so an archive
  over either is refused with the file, both numbers and the cap in the error and is never opened;
  the post-open entry count stays as a second line of defence. `sniff_path()` now asks
  `is_zip_file()` instead of opening the archive, which is cheaper and keeps the refusal with its
  numbers on the path the user is really on: the registry still dispatches the file to this reader,
  so the import says what was wrong with the archive rather than that nothing recognised it. Tests
  in `SlicedArchiveResinReaderTests.cpp` (2 cases, 6 sections): the record of a plain zip, a comment
  that carries a signature of its own, a zip64 record, a file that is not a zip; and the refusals -
  500000 entries in an archive of one, a 3 GB central directory, the same promise in a zip64 record,
  all three through `read()` (and the first through the registry as well, with `sniff_path()` still
  claiming the file), against an archive with a comment inside the caps that imports as before. The
  user guide's archive paragraph and its known limits carry the numbers. NOT BUILT when it was
  written; a human builds and runs the suites. Fix 2026-10-01 (found by a test build): the "more entries than the cap" case asked the record of a plain zip to declare 500000 entries, but that record counts them in 16 bits, so raw_zip() truncated the claim to 41248 and the refusal named that number, which is what the reader found. The case declares 40000 entries now: over the cap of 16384 and the most a plain record can state. The zip64 section keeps a count of 200000, since a zip64 record is the only way one that big is stated. No product change.

The units are M3.10c's own (the spellings `ConfigDefsSLA.cpp` gives the same quantity) and stay untranslated, because a report has to read the same in every language; a key that is not a quantity, or one whose unit M3.1/M3.2 have not settled, carries an empty string rather than a guess, and a row that writes nothing still carries the unit of the value the file had. The pinned two-file document in `ResinImportReportTests.cpp` is updated for all eight of its rows and a fourth case covers the units of all four shapes (a converted speed, a copied value, a key that is not a quantity, a row that writes nothing) against the report sorted by key, so a unit is a pinned part of the document rather than an extra nobody reads. The doc comment of `resin_import_report_json()` and the report sample in the user guide say the same. Tests only, plus the two report writers; NOT BUILT when it was written, a human builds and runs the suites.
  Left: a unit is still absent for a key the mapping table has no settled unit for (a layer count, a PWM level, a price in a currency nobody has confirmed), so a script reading such a row still gets a bare number; the units themselves are the mapping table's own and are not confirmed against a real file (M3.1/M3.2).

A setting the datasheet does not state is left out of the profile instead of written as a zero, and a price per bottle is written as the per-litre price of that same bottle, so the mapper's bottle cost is the price again and both numbers stay in the report. The interactor gained `import_profile()`, and everything from picking the base to saving the preset is now the one `import_read_profile()` that `import_file()` and it share, so the only thing the form needed on top of that is an `open(ForeignResinProfile)` overload of the review dialog; `material_source_note` for such a preset says "Datasheet, entered <date>" instead of naming a file. Also fixed here: neither `ResinImportDialog` nor the new form was inserted into the dialog navigation, so `Navigator::set_opened_dialog()` closed every dialog instead of opening them, which is why the M3.10 review dialog could not be shown at all; both are now children of the material dialog in the Plater and the Preview render module. NOT BUILT when it was written; a human builds and runs the suites.
  Left: the form asks for the six values a datasheet states and the three the material settings have keys for, so the lift, retract and transition-layer values a generic MSLA datasheet may also give are not asked for; the validation checks that a number is a positive number but not the range the option itself allows, so a datasheet outside it is written and shown; the review row for the price names a per-litre price where the datasheet said per bottle (its note carries both numbers).
- [x] **M3.11b** The datasheet form checks that a number is a positive number but not the range the option it writes allows, so a datasheet outside that range is written and shown: validate every field against the limits its `sla_material_settings` option declares and name the field that is out of range. · S · needs M3.11
  Result: `validate_datasheet()` now checks every number of the form against the `<min, max>` of the option it is written to, read out of `ConfigDefsSLA.cpp` through `Domain::get_defs_sla()` rather than repeated in the form, so a datasheet outside the range of its setting is refused before it reaches the review table instead of being written and shown there. Each rule of `ResinDatasheetForm.cpp` names the `sla_material_settings` key the mapping table writes its key to, and the value is compared **in the unit that option holds**: a rule carries how many units of the setting one unit of the form is worth, which is 1 for everything but the lift and retract speeds (asked in mm/min, written in mm/s, so divided by 60), or a limit of the setting would be applied to the wrong number. The message is a new pair, *"{field} must be {limit} or more."* and *"... or less."*, with the limit spelled the way the config spells it and the unit the option declares, so the user is told which number to change and by how much; the existing messages for a missing, non-numeric or non-positive value are untouched and still come first. A bottle below the 50 ml `bottle_volume` takes and a light-off delay above the 30 s `delay_before_exposure` takes are the two limits this build actually has, and both are now refused with the field named. The bottom layer count is checked against `bottom_layer_count`, the one a printer that lifts the build plate keeps the count in, because the 3 to 20 a tilt printer clamps it into is the mapper's doing and its row already says so. `ResinDatasheetFormTests.cpp` grows four cases: every field's option exists, is a material option and declares a minimum; the mapping table is asked where each key of the form goes and has to agree with the rule, including the price that needs `resinUnit` beside it and the bottle volume that is read but written nowhere; every field that has a maximum refuses double it with the field and the limit in the message and accepts the limit itself; and the bottle is refused below the smallest one. The field table in the user guide carries the new rules. NOT BUILT when it was written; a human builds and runs the suites. Fix 2026-10-01 (found by a test build): the table of fields in the test carried a flag for a setting the mapping table does not write, which was written as false for a bottle volume before M3.15b and is not any more, so that field was checked against an empty target key and failed. The flag is gone: every field of the form is checked against the setting its rule names, the bottle volume included, which is bottle_volume. The comment on the bottle volume rule in ResinDatasheetForm.cpp said the mapping table writes none of its own and is corrected.
  Left: only two of the eleven fields have a limit this build can act on, because `ConfigDefsSLA.cpp` gives the rest a minimum of 0 and no maximum, so a datasheet with a 400 s exposure is still accepted; the bottom layer count is only checked against the generic MSLA range, since the form does not know which printer the preset is for, and the tilt printer's own 3 to 20 clamp stays the mapper's; and a rule whose option this build does not have checks nothing, which the first new test case is what would notice.

The number on the left of the row is 25 for a datasheet that said 25, the number on the right is 25, and the conversion is still auditable in the note. The assumed 1 litre bottle of the form is gone; that assumption is the mapper's, where it already was for a file.   Result: the reverse of the M3.5/M3.6 table is `Biz/ResinProfile/ChituboxCfgExport.{hpp,cpp}`: `export_chitubox_cfg(material, printer class, name)` writes the keys `ChituboxCfgReader` reads, one "key: value" per line under a comment header, and `export_chitubox_cfg_report()` returns the same text plus a report with a row per key of the table - what was written, and for a setting Chitubox has no key for (bottle_cost, material_vendor, use_tilt, the lift keys on a tilt printer) a row that says why and is listed in `skipped`. Units go back the other way (mm/s -> mm/min for the lift, retract and drop speeds), a [below, above] area fill pair is written as the one value Chitubox has (the one below the threshold) with the other named in the note, a value of zero is not written because zero means the setting is unused, and `export_printer_class()` decides the table from the printer model and the preset's use_tilt, the same way the import does, so a preset that goes out and comes back is mapped the same way both times. `ChituboxCfgExportTests.cpp` (registered in `CMakeLists.txt`) has 9 cases: the keys of a generic MSLA preset, the speeds converted back, the tilt table (no lift keys, the fade as transition layers, the wait before a lift as a delay after the exposure), the settings left out and reported, zero, the area fill pair, a preset name that cannot break the line it is on, the printer class, and the round trip: a hand-written fixture .cfg -> import (dry run mapping, and the mapper directly for the tilt class) -> export -> the reader reads it back and every mapped key comes back with the value the file gave it. On the CLI it is `--export-resin-profile <preset-name> --output <file.cfg>` next to M3.9's `--import-resin-profile` (`Init.hpp`, `ReadCLI.cpp`, `ProcessActions.cpp`): the preset is looked up by name or id among the resins of the selected printer, and the settings the file has no key for are named in the console. NOT BUILT when it was written. Fix 2026-10-01 (found by a test build): the row loop of "a setting with no Chitubox key is left out and named" asserted that a row writes a value exactly when it writes a unit, but only a price is stated as two keys (resinPrice and resinUnit), so every other written row fails that. The loop checks what its comment says instead: a row that writes nothing carries no unit, and a row carries a unit and a unit value together. Fix 2026-10-01 (found by a test build): check_round_trip() exported under the fixed name "Round trip resin" and then asked that the name read back be the one the original file states, which is "Tilt resin" in the tilt section, so the name could only fail. The helper reads the original first and exports under the name that file states, so the name really makes the trip; a file that states no name is still exported under the name of the round trip, and then there is nothing to compare with.
  Left: the file is a resin profile, not a print profile: a real .cfg also carries the machine's G-code, its model and its build volume, and none of that is a resin setting, so the file is meant to be read as a resin rather than opened as a print; the price of a bottle cannot go back at all, since a .cfg states a price per litre and the bottle it was made for is not a resin setting; there is no UI button, which M3.15 leaves out on purpose.
