Fix 2026-10-01 (found by a test build): export_preset_to_file() opened the .cfg in text mode, so on Windows every line of the file ended in CRLF and the file on disk was not the text the report describes: the case that reads the file back and compares it with result.exported.text failed on the line endings alone. The file is written in binary now, as the .ctb, .goo and .pwmx writers and the yaml files already are, and the reader of a .cfg normalises line endings before it parses.

## M4: Engine quality (measure first; every PR includes before/after metrics)

Support generation quality has its own milestone, **M7**. M4.3–M4.5 cover regression tests and structural fixes; M7 measures quality against expert-made supports.

- [ ] **M4.1** Tracy profiling run over the benchmark set. Write a hotspot report in `doc/sla-fork/profiling/`. No code changes. · M · needs M0.13
- [ ] **M4.2** `[human]` Re-rank M4.3–M4.10 based on the M4.1 report. · S · needs M4.1
- [ ] **M4.3** Support point generation: regression tests for `tests/data/sla_islands/*.svg` and the benchmark set (PLAN B2). · M · needs M4.1
  - [x] **M4.3a** The island fixtures as a regression test; the hanging `overhang.obj` case hidden. · M · needs —
    Result: `tests/sla_print/sla_island_coverage_tests.cpp` runs the `tests/data/sla_islands/*.svg` fixtures through the pipeline; `overhang.obj` is marked as a hidden case because the support generator hangs on it. Tested: both suites green 2026-09-30.
  - [x] **M4.3b** Split the island coverage test into per-fixture cases and add a hidden local model case. · M · needs M4.3a
    Result: the test is table-driven per fixture, plus a case for a model that is kept outside the repo (the M7 dataset rule) and hidden from the default run. Tested: both suites green 2026-09-30.
  - [x] **M4.3c** Make the reported local-minimum miss measurable on the real local models: a hidden test that runs the support point generator over the benchmark corpus and lists every island (M4.8d rule) that no support point covers, with the local minima in a second list. · M · needs M4.3b
    Result: `tests/sla_print/sla_local_island_coverage_tests.cpp`, hidden `[.local]` and skipped unless `SLA_LOCAL_SAMPLES` points at the local-samples folder. It reads `<folder>/benchmark models/manifest.yaml` through the new `sla_bench_manifest.hpp` (the manifest reader, the plate placement and the path-scrubbing moved there, shared with the M0.13 harness, so both read the corpus the same way) and runs the ids of `SLA_ISLAND_IDS` (default bm01,bm13, the two helmets). Per model: placed on the plate as the benchmark does, `sla::generate_support_points_for_tool` on the default config, the model sliced with the generator's own heights, closing radius and slicing mode, and then every island of the M4.8d rule with no support point inside the coverage radius (0.5 mm in Z, sqrt(area_mm2) + 1 mm in XY) listed as id, layer index, Z, area and centroid, with a `CHECK` on the count being 0 so a miss fails visibly. The local minima are the uncovered areas the M4.8d rule does not call islands whose area grows for at least 2 layers upwards (the walk is capped at 8 layers); they are reported with INFO and as a second list, not asserted on. Only manifest ids are ever printed, never a file name or a path. **Not built or run here**, the human does it: `set SLA_LOCAL_SAMPLES=local-samples`, then `build-default\tests\sla_print\Release\sla_print_tests.exe "[.local]"`. Suspected causes of the 2026-09-28 report, from reading the generator (not changed in this job): a region whose 3D bounding sphere is under `minimal_bounding_sphere_radius` (0.2 mm, `SLA/SupportIslands/SampleConfig.hpp:36`) is dropped whole by `get_small_parts`/`erase` in `SLA/SupportPointGenerator.cpp:1042`; `move_on_mesh_surface` (`SLA/SupportPointGenerator.cpp:1609`) replaces a point with the closest mesh point when the vertical ray misses by more than one layer height, which can slide it off the region it was sampled for; the zero-elevation filter in `SLASupportTool.cpp:210` drops every point at the model's z = 0; and a region that is not an island is only sampled where `sample_overhangs` finds an overhang contour, filtered by the `min_part_length` and outline distances of `SLA/SupportIslands/SampleConfigFactory.cpp:60`.
  Left: M4.3 itself is still open: the island fixtures are covered, but the benchmark set cannot run until M4.1 (the profiling run) and M0.13 (the benchmark harness) are done.
- [ ] **M4.4** Support point generation: fix the worst unsupported-island cases found in M4.3. · L → split · needs M4.3
  - [x] **M4.4a** Measure the four suspected causes of the reported auto support miss (the list the M4.3c result note leaves behind) on a mesh built in code for each, and fix the ones that are real. · M · needs —
    Result: `tests/sla_print/sla_support_regression_tests.cpp` (registered in `tests/sla_print/CMakeLists.txt`), 5 cases on meshes written in the test, each run through `sla::generate_support_points_for_tool` on the default config at the 0.05 mm layer height of the benchmark harness and then through the island coverage rule of `sla_island_coverage_tests.cpp` (every island of the M4.8d rule, an area of at least `SLA::min_island_area_mm2` with nothing below it, has a point within 0.5 mm in Z and sqrt(area_mm2) + 1 mm in XY). The local minima of M4.3c are deliberately not asserted: a local minimum overlaps the layer below and hangs on the material under it (a valley in the outside of a dome, the bottom of a cavity), so a support point there is resin in a self supporting corner, and the list stays a list. Per cause: **(1) the small parts erase, REPRODUCED and fixed** - a 0.38 mm disc floating in mid air next to a block is collected by `get_small_parts()` (its bounding box is inside the 0.4 mm the erase looks for) and dropped whole, so the tool made no point under it at all, and the app reports the same 0.11 mm2 speck as an island that can fall off. The fix keeps a small part the island rule reports: `prepare_generator_data` filters the small parts with a new `is_reportable_island()` (an island of the M4.8d rule, so a part with empty `prev_parts`, whose area reaches one threshold) before `erase()`, and the threshold is the one the island detector already uses, moved from a local constant in `SLAPrintSteps.cpp` to `SLA::min_island_area_mm2` in `SLA/IslandDetection.hpp` so the two rules cannot drift apart. Specks under the threshold are still erased, and a small part that is *connected* to the model was never erased anyway (the flood fill of `create_small_part()` walks out of the sphere into the body), which the second case keeps that way with a 0.4 mm spike hanging under a brim. **(2) `move_on_mesh_surface()`, NOT reproduced** - a 4 x 4 mm plate floating 1.7 mm under a 30 mm brim is exactly the geometry the report describes, and the point of its first layer keeps its place: the ray down finds the plate's own bottom face 0.025 mm below, and every point in the plate's own column comes out on the plate (the brim is 1.975 mm above it). The generator can only feed this step a point that is on a surface or within a layer of one - an island point sits at a layer whose layer below is empty in its own column, an overhang sample lies on the contour it was sampled from - so the closest point branch, the one that could carry a point to another feature, is not reached; the step only ever moves a point onto its own surface. **(3) the zero elevation filter of `SLASupportTool.cpp`, NOT reproduced** - the filter drops points at the model's z = 0 within `Domain::EPSILON` (0.1 um), while a support point always sits at a layer centre, at least half a layer above the plate; a 1.5 mm plate hovering 0.5 mm above the plate with the raft around the object keeps its point. **(4) the overhang thresholds of `SampleConfigFactory`, NOT reproduced** - `min_part_length` and the outline distances belong to the island sampling, which only sees parts with nothing below them, so they cannot touch a region that is not an island; an overhang is sampled along its whole contour whatever its width, at `discretize_overhang_step` (2 mm) with a sample inside the influence radius of a point already made dropped, and a 6 mm overhang on a 14 mm plate has a point every 3 mm along its edge (unsampled, the nearest point would be 8 mm away under the post). Only cause 1 changed anything; the other three are measured, not touched. NOT BUILT, a human build and run is needed.
- [ ] **M4.5** Branching tree: reduce support volume with no new unsupported points (PLAN B3). · L → split · needs M4.1
  - [x] **M4.5a** The volume lever itself, behind a config key: how much fatter than the head it carries a branch may get, plus the volume and invariant tests on a sphere and a helmet-like dome. · M · needs —
    Result: the volume driver of the branching tree is the widening in `BranchingTreeSLA.cpp`: a node is built with `Rmin + 0.05 * pillar_widening_factor * weight`, and the weight of a node is the length of the longest chain of branches merged into it, so a trunk is as fat as the whole subtree it holds up and the pillar of a tall model is the fattest thing in the print. The new key `branchingsupport_pillar_radius_cap` (hidden like the other branching keys, `0` = off) caps that at a multiple of the radius of the head the branch carries, through `SupportTreeConfig::pillar_radius_cap`, read by `make_support_cfg` for the branching tree only. The cap is measured from the `Rmin` of the node, which is the largest head radius above it, so it can only cut the widening and never the branch below the head it serves: a cap under one is read as one and no value of the key can make a branch thinner than its head. The formula moved out of the builder class into `sla::branch_radius()` in `BranchingTreeSLA.hpp` so that a test can call it, and at 0 it is the expression of before, term for term, so the default is the tree of today. `SupportTreeBuilder` grew `diffbridges()` and `junctions()` so that a test can read the radii of the branches.
    Tests: `tests/sla_print/sla_branching_volume_tests.cpp` (registered in `CMakeLists.txt`, 4 cases) writes two models in code - a 20 mm sphere standing on the plate, and a helmet-like dome, a hemisphere on a stem - puts 18 support points on the surface of each and builds every one of them twice, with the cap off and on. Per case: the fattest branch of the tree of today is above the cap (or the cap would have nothing to take out and the case would prove nothing), with the cap on no branch, pillar or junction of the tree is fatter than the cap, the resin of the tree is less (the volume of its primitives summed, which is what the cap changes), no head that had a branch loses it, none is left without one by more of them than before, and every connected head has a branch at least as fat as the head itself. The other two cases are the formula (0 is today's radius, a cap no radius reaches changes nothing, any other cap is the widening off at that factor and never below `Rmin`) and the def of the key. `sla_invalidation_tests.cpp` carries the new key in its three lists. NOT BUILT, a human build and run is needed.
    Left, and the M4.5b A/B has to know it: the tree is not the same from one run to the next, so two runs of the same model cannot be compared to the last digit. The model and bed points the tree merges branches between are drawn from `std::rand` (libigl's `random_points_on_mesh` on Eigen's generator, `BranchingTree/PointCloud.cpp`), and the per leaf ground searches run on several threads over one global NLopt generator (`Optimizer::seed` is `nlopt_srand`), so the search results race as well; the test seeds `std::rand` before each build, which fixes the first half only. Also found while reading and not changed here, both outside the volume question: `create_branching_tree` gives up on a head with `builder.head(id)` where `id` is the index of the leaf in the point cloud while `m_head_indices` is keyed by the index of the support point, so the head that is dropped is the wrong one as soon as two points are closer to each other than `non_duplicate_suppt_indices` allows or one point got no head of its own; and the cap thins the beams the tree is routed with, so a capped tree may connect heads the tree of today gave up on, which is why the cases assert that no head is lost rather than that the count is the same.
  - [ ] **M4.5b** A/B the key and the existing widening factor on the benchmark set with the M0.13 harness, record the support volume before and after, and say which default the presets get. · M · needs M4.5a, M0.13, M4.1
  - [x] **M4.5c** Make the branching tree reproducible from one run to the next, and give every head the id of the support point it was built for (the two things the M4.5a result note leaves behind). · M · needs M4.5a
    Result: the tree of one model is now the same tree twice, down to the last bit of every vertex. Three sources of run to run movement, all in the engine, and one of bookkeeping that the note found. **(1) the sampled points** - the bed and model points the tree merges branches between were drawn by libigl's `random_points_on_mesh` off Eigen's generator, which is `std::rand` (`BranchingTree/PointCloud.cpp`), so they were different in every process; the same Turk's method (a triangle with the probability of its share of the surface, then a point uniformly inside it) is drawn now from a `std::mt19937_64` seeded with an FNV-1a hash of the mesh, of the sampling radius and of which surface is sampled, so the points are a function of the input and the libigl call is gone. The uniform draw is arithmetic rather than a distribution, whose output the standard does not fix. **(2) the searches** - `Optimizer::seed()` called `nlopt_srand()`, which seeds the one process wide generator every running NLopt algorithm draws from, so the per leaf ground searches (and the pinhead and anchor searches, `SupportTreeUtils.hpp:512, 816, 997`) raced each other and their result followed the scheduling; `seed()` now only remembers the seed and `NLoptOpt::optimize()` seeds NLopt and holds a mutex (`nlopt_rng_lock()`) from the seeding to the end of `nlopt_optimize()`, so every search starts from its own seed and runs to the end before the next one starts. What is given up is the parallelism between the searches of different leaves, not inside one of them: the ray casts of a beam are still parallel. An optimizer that never asked for a seed is seeded with 0 rather than with the time, so it is reproducible too. **(3) the order the candidates are tried in** - `build_tree` sorted them by the distance to branch at with `std::sort` alone, which leaves two candidates at the same distance in whatever order the point cloud handed them over, so a tie is broken by node id now. **(4) the heads** - `calculate_pinhead_placement` sets the id of the support point it was calculated for, and `create_branching_tree` overwrote it with the number of the leaf; the leaf of a head is not that support point, since a point that gets no head of its own and a point that is dropped as the duplicate of a point next to it both move the leaves off the support points, so a head asked for by the number of a leaf is the head of another point from there on, and the tree gave up on such a head instead of on the right one. Heads are added under the id of their support point, the unroutable leaves are sorted and deduplicated before any head is given up, and a head is looked up by the id of the support point its leaf came from. `SupportTreeBuilder` keeps `m_head_indices` sparse-safe for it: a new `no_head` sentinel marks the slots of the ids no head was added under, `head_of(id)` returns nullptr for those and `has_head(id)` says so, and `head(id)` asserts on the sentinel.
    Tests: `tests/sla_print/sla_branching_determinism_tests.cpp` (registered in `CMakeLists.txt`, 4 cases) writes a 20 mm sphere in code with twelve support points on its lower third, and: the same tree is built five times over and the vertex and face data of the merged support meshes are compared with the bits of the floats, so a tree that moved by a single epsilon fails and the hash in the INFO is only there to say which build moved; the tree is built once more with `tbb::global_control` pinning the process to one thread and compared with the first; the sphere is built twice more with a pair of points 0.05 mm apart added to it, of which the tree keeps one and drops the other as its duplicate (`non_duplicate_suppt_indices`), and with a point at 170 degrees of the sphere where the surface does not face down and no head fits; and every support point of that last model is asked for its head by its own id - a point that got no head has none to ask for, and the head of every other id sits where `calculate_pinhead_placement` puts it and carries that id, which is what the number of a leaf cannot say. `sla_branching_volume_tests.cpp` no longer seeds `std::rand` before a build and its note says the tree is reproducible. NOT BUILT, a human build and run is needed.
    Left: the searches of the different leaves no longer overlap in time, so a support tree is likely a little slower to build; if a run of the benchmark set shows it matters, the per leaf search is what has to be parallel again, each search with a generator of its own, which NLopt does not offer. M4.5b may now A/B the key to the last digit.

  - [x] **M4.6a** The robustness half: pad and raft generation with the object printed directly on the plate (zero elevation, raft around the object, supports on and off), for every raft type and with the edge taper on and off. · M · needs —
    Result: `tests/sla_print/sla_pad_robustness_tests.cpp` (registered in `CMakeLists.txt`) builds six awkward footprints in code - a 2 mm ring, two blocks 5 mm apart (rafts that should merge), a plate with a 3 mm slot, a 1 mm cube, a C, and a ball that touches the plate at one point - and runs each of them through the support tool (`generate_support_points_for_tool` then `build_support_tree_for_tool`) and through a full `SLAPrint` slice, both on a worker thread with a deadline, for `raft_type` None / Full / Around object / Skate, `raft_edge_taper` 0 and 1, and supports on and off. Asserted per case: no crash; a closed raft wherever one is generated (every edge shared by exactly two faces of opposite winding); the object's bottom layer inside the raft's outline and not buried in it (a raft around the object) or the raft under the supports that hold the object up (a raft under it); the elevation the object is printed at; and the documented no-raft paths - an empty raft for a raft-around-the-object type that does not follow the object, `NoPadGenerated` when a raft is required but its wall does not fit, and the cavity the pad logs and drops. Three fixes in the pad generator, all found by reading the on-plate path: `pad_blueprint` simplified the degenerate contour a slicing plane that only grazes the mesh leaves (the bottom of a ball standing on the plate is exactly such a plane), and `ExPolygon::simplify` reads the first point of the contour; `offset_contour_only` took the first piece of an inward offset that splits a raft part, which is not an outline of that part and left the raft with a hole in it, and now drops the part (the caller keeps the sharp edge or skips the part); `ConcaveHull::add_connector_rectangles` divided by the distance between an island's centroid and the centre of the islands, which is zero when two islands share one, and handed Clipper a vertex that is not a number. NOT BUILT (no build in this session).
  Progress 2026-09-30: the robustness half of M4.6 is M4.6a above; the speed half stays unticked because it needs M4.1 (the Tracy run) before any step time can be recorded. Three facts the cases had to be written around, all now covered by tests rather than changed: with the object on the plate the supports are heads on the object that reach nothing, so a raft around the object only exists with "raft around object everywhere" on - with it off every raft piece is judged redundant against support footprints that are all inside the object, and the raft comes out empty (documented, not an error); a raft *under* an object that stands on the plate has no pillars to stand on and is refused with `NoPadGenerated`, so a raft under the object asks for an elevation (5 mm in the tests); and with the default Skate values the frame around the object is as wide as the connector cavities cut into it (half the 1.6 mm expansion against a 1 mm object gap), so the cases set `pad_brim_size` to 4 mm (Skate halves it) to have a raft to test. Left: the speed half, and a decision on whether a raft around the object on the plate should need the everywhere option at all.

  Progress: the test half is M4.7a below and is in; the performance half (hollowing time and peak memory over the benchmark set, before and after) is still open, because it needs the M4.1 profiling run to say what to measure.
  - [x] **M4.7a** The test half: hollow generated meshes at several `hollowing_min_thickness` / `hollowing_quality` / `hollowing_closing_distance` settings and measure the wall that comes out. · M · needs —
    Result: `tests/sla_print/sla_hollowing_tests.cpp` (2 cases, registered in `tests/sla_print/CMakeLists.txt`) hollows four models written in code — a 20mm sphere, a 20mm cube, a cylinder with a 10mm thin neck and an L block with a sharp inner corner — at 1, 2 and 3mm walls, and the sphere and the L block additionally at the accuracy extremes (quality 0 and 1, closing distance 0 and 0.5). The wall is measured by sampling the centroids of the generated interior and taking the squared distance to the outer model with `AABBMesh`; every case reports its min, mean, max, the voxel size and the tolerance with `INFO`, so a regression shows how far it moved. The bound is `min_thickness - (closing_distance + 2 * voxel)`, from the two things that can move the inner surface towards the outer one: the interior is a marching cubes extraction of the voxel SDF and so sits within about a voxel of the true offset surface, and the closing distance rounds the interior with a ball of that radius.
    A case exposed a bug and the fix is in the same commit: `mesh_to_grid` voxelizes a mesh scaled up by the voxel scale and scales the grid transform back afterwards, so the distance values stored in the grid count voxels, but `generate_interior` passed `min_thickness` and `closing_distance` to `redistance_grid` / `grid_to_mesh` and compared raw grid distances against millimetres as if they were millimetres. Since `2144f81bf1` moved the voxel scaling out of the caller and into `mesh_to_grid` the offset has been `1/voxel_scale` of the requested wall (0.4mm instead of 2mm at the same settings, 0.65mm instead of the 3mm config default), so the wall thickness was set by the quality and closing distance rather than by `hollowing_min_thickness`; the 1.1x narrow band margin, which only means something if the offset is a distance, says the same. `Hollowing.cpp` now converts the two isovalues and the band width to voxel units, divides the two grid distance getters by the voxel scale and keeps that scale in `Interior`. The min/mean/max per case are printed by the test, not recorded here: NOT BUILT, a human build and run is still needed before this half can be called green.

  - [x] **M4.8e** Cup and trapped-resin detection on the sliced layers, after the slice: every region of empty space that is enclosed by solid in its layer and stays enclosed going upward until a layer closes it (a suction cup, PLAN B5b), and every cavity closed at both ends (trapped resin), each reported as a `SlaIssue{Kind::Cup}` / `SlaIssue{Kind::TrappedResin}` with its layer range, opening area and volume. No drain hole suggestions yet, that is a later sub-item. · M · needs M4.8b
    Result: `SLA/CavityDetection.{hpp,cpp}` (the PLAN B5b `SLA/CavityAnalysis.*`, named after `IslandDetection` like its neighbours) with one pure `detect_cavities(layers, layer_thicknesses_mm, opts)`, called from `merge_slices_and_eval_stats()` right after `detect_islands()` on the same merged layer polygons, so the M1.11b sidebar list (which already lists cups) and the M5.8 markers pick the kinds up; the M4.8c notification stays islands only, as its own tests require. `SLAResult.hpp` needed no edit: `Kind::Cup` and `Kind::TrappedResin` were already reserved. Definitions, in the header and as comments: a CUP is a hole of the ExPolygons of a layer that stays a hole going up until a layer covers it and has no solid under its bottom layer, so it is an upside-down pocket that pins a vacuum against the vat film on every peel; it reports the layer range, the opening area (the part of the first layer's hole with no solid under it) and the volume (hole area x layer thickness, summed). TRAPPED RESIN is a hole closed under as well, an enclosed cavity with no path to the outside in 3D, which is what a hollow print without a drain hole leaves; it reports the volume and the layer range. One walk over the layers, linear in the layer count: the holes of a layer (every hole of the layer's ExPolygons less the solid standing inside one of them, so a post in a pocket is not counted as pocket) are matched against the regions carried over from the layer below with a union-find over the overlapping ones, so a pocket that splits and merges again stays one pocket. Thresholds, both in `CavityDetectionOptions`: a cup opening under `min_cup_opening_mm2` (1 mm2) is ignored, and a pocket open below through less than that is reported as neither, too small to seal against the film and narrow enough to drain, which is how a drain hole keeps a hollow print quiet; a cavity closed on every side counts as trapped resin from `min_trapped_volume_mm3` (1 mm3) up. 13 tests in `tests/sla_print/sla_cavity_detection_tests.cpp` over generated layer geometry: an upside-down cup lifted off the plate on two uprights (64 mm2 opening, 192 mm3 over layers 1 to 3), the same cup with a vent hole through the roof (nothing), a hollow cube (216 mm3 of trapped resin), the same cube with a drain hole through its floor (nothing), a solid cube (nothing), three separate cups (three), the 1 mm2 threshold and a lowered one, the layer thickness in the volume, a post inside the pocket, a pocket that vents above, two cavities merging into one, a cavity that widens upward, and empty input. The issue note carries the numbers and `issue.layer` is the first layer of the range; the layer numbers in the notes are the 0 based slice indices, the sidebar counts them from one. Left, as the todo said: the drain hole suggestions, so no position and nothing to accept. Two limits worth knowing: the layers are merged, so a support that fills a pocket hides it (conservative, it never invents a problem), and a pocket open to the side (a notch cut from the bottom) is not a hole of a layer and is not reported. NOT BUILT, a human build and run is needed.

- [x] **M4.8h** Suggest hollowing where it saves real resin: after a slice (never before, never slicing on its own), for each object that is NOT hollowed and whose cured volume is above a threshold (a new setting `hollowing_suggest_min_volume`, ml, default 20, 0 = off) and whose shape can take a wall (its smallest cross-section dimension at mid height > 2 x hollowing_min_thickness + some margin, the rule documented in the header), add a notification line: "<model> could be hollowed: about N ml of resin saved" (estimate: the volume minus the volume of a shell of hollowing_min_thickness, from the slices' areas and perimeters: area - perimeter x wall per layer), with an action "Open hollowing" that opens the hollowing tool on that model (like "Edit supports" opens the support tool). Hollowed models and FFF are skipped. Pure function for the estimate and the eligibility with Catch2 tests. · M · needs M4.8, M2.4
  Result: the App half on top of the engine half of the same commit. `App/SlaHollowingSuggestion.{hpp,cpp}` (next to `SlaIssueRows.hpp`, named for the header the engine half points at) holds the pure `hollowing_saved_ml()`, `hollowing_suggestion_eligible()`, `suggest_hollowing()`, `hollowing_settings()`, `hollowing_suggestion_text()` and `build_hollowing_suggestion_notification()`, then the `SlaHollowingNotification` listener that puts them on screen, and the new `PopNotificationType::SlaHollowingSuggestion` it closes by name. The numbers come from `SLAResultData::object_slice_stats` of the slice that just finished and from the config of that same slice (`hollowing_suggest_min_volume` and `hollowing_min_thickness` are read off it by `hollowing_settings()`, so a print sliced before a setting was changed is never judged against a value it was not made with), so nothing is ever sliced to make a suggestion: the listener runs off `StatusCode::Finished` and nothing else. THE RULE, in the header: not hollowed, a threshold above zero, at least the threshold of resin of its own, a cross section at half the height, and that cross section wider than two walls plus `hollowing_suggest_margin_mm` (1 mm, a third of the default wall and larger than the default closing distance, so a shape at the line is not one click from an unsound shell) - a wall is offset inwards from the surface on both sides of the shell, so the width it fits into is twice the wall. FFF is skipped inside `suggest_hollowing()` on the printer technology rather than in the caller, and the listener refuses a non-SLA printer before it reads a config that has neither key. THE ESTIMATE is the one the todo names, the shell of a layer being its outline times the wall, and it is an UPPER bound: two offset surfaces meeting in a corner cut away the same resin twice, so a solid 40 mm cube reads about 45 ml saved against the 17.7 ml a 3 mm shell really leaves. That is the safe direction for a suggestion (it never says a hollow print saves less than it does) and the notification says "about" for the same reason; the test pins both numbers so a change to either shows up. One notification per model, each with its own "Open hollowing" button, because the models are separate decisions and the hollowing tool takes one model at a time; the button selects the model and then activates `Scene::ToolType::SlaHollow` through the navigator, exactly the order of `SidebarSlaSupports::open_support_tool()`, and it hollows and slices nothing itself. The offers close on the same events the M4.8c issue notification closes on (a new slice starting, the project changing, the project closing), and a model that has left the plate since the slice is not offered at all, because there would be no tool to open on it. Wiring: two lines in the Plater module (the include, a forward declaration and one `make_unique`), the navigator read through the callback because the module is given one after it is built. `resources/localization/list.txt` gets the new file, or its strings never reach translators. 8 Catch2 cases (14 sections) in `test/Slic3r/App/SlaHollowingSuggestionTests.cpp` over blocks written in the test: a solid 40 mm cube (64 ml, about 45 ml saved, offered), the same cube hollowed (not offered), a 3 mm plate laid flat (4.8 ml, under the threshold, not offered), a 3 mm plate stood on edge (30 ml, over the threshold, skipped for its 3 mm cross section), a 7 mm and a 7.5 mm cross section either side of the two-walls-plus-margin line, a model with no cross section at half its height, a model under the threshold, a threshold of zero against the same cube that the default threshold offers, FFF against SLA, the text with and without a model name, the notification with its one button pressed, and the two keys read off a real `FullConfigSLA` config view and off one that has no values. NOT BUILT: a human build and run is needed. Known limit, said rather than hidden: the wall is the print-level `hollowing_min_thickness`, so a model that overrides the key for itself alone is judged by the print value.

Tests in `tests/sla_print/sla_layer_stats_tests.cpp` (SLA suite 48 -> 55 cases). The peel force is area times a placeholder constant, documented as such: only relative values are meaningful until someone measures a real printer. Nothing displays these yet (M5.7).
  - [x] **M4.9b** The peel force of a layer becomes a model a user can tune instead of area times a placeholder constant: F = k_area * cured area + k_perimeter * boundary length + k_suction * the area of the cup openings M4.8e found open on that layer, with the coefficients of the vat film (`vat_film_type`, FEP / nFEP / PFA / ACF, default FEP) and optional `peel_area_coefficient` / `peel_perimeter_coefficient` overrides (0 = from the film). The coefficients and where they come from are written up in doc/sla-fork/profiling/peel-force.md, as rough figures from the literature and not as measurements. `peel_force_warning` (N, negative = the default of the film, 0 = off) turns every layer above it into a `SlaIssue` of the new kind `HighPeelForce`, and the M5.6c chart names the film in its title and writes the limit under it. · M · needs M4.9, M4.8e
    Result: `SLA/LayerStats.{hpp,cpp}` (moved from the private `src` to the public `include` of libslic3r so the app can read the model too) holds the whole model and no config reading: `peel_force_coefficients(film)`, `PeelForceSettings::coefficients()` for the two overrides, `peel_force_warning_n(setting, k)`, `layer_perimeters_mm()` (contour plus hole lengths of the same ExPolygons the area comes from), `peel_force_estimate()` and `layers_over_peel_force()`. Defaults: FEP 0.10 N/mm2 and 0.15 N/mm with a 20 N warning, nFEP 0.08 / 0.12 / 18, PFA 0.06 / 0.10 / 15, ACF 0.02 / 0.04 / 8, and k_suction 2 to 3 times k_area because a cup seals against the film. They are placeholders on the low end of the published ranges on purpose, so a layer that is really fine is not warned about. The cup term reuses the M4.8e `CupHit`s: `cup_suction_area_mm2()` in `SLA/CavityDetection.*` adds the opening of every cup to each layer it is open for (a trapped cavity adds nothing, it holds no vacuum). `merge_slices_and_eval_stats()` reads the four keys, fills `layer_peel_force` as before and adds one `HighPeelForce` issue per high layer; `SLAResult.hpp` grew one enumerator. The layer window shows the film in the chart title and, on a high layer, "Peel 24.1 N (over 20.0 N)" under it. 15 cases in `tests/sla_print/sla_layer_stats_tests.cpp` (the placeholder ones rewritten): a comb of ten 1 mm teeth costs more than the 10x10 mm square of the same 100 mm2 (220 mm of boundary against 40 mm), FEP above ACF on the same layers, every film finite and positive, the cups adding their suction term, a threshold of zero (and a negative one) flagging nothing, and a layer exactly at the limit not counted. The four new keys got their `invalidated_by` entries and joined the list of `sla_invalidation_tests.cpp`. NOT BUILT: a human build and run is needed before this can be called green.

    Result: restored by OpenCode; the compile errors were untangled by hand (2026-09-27): the header now includes libslic3r's `Point.hpp` for `Slic3r::Vec2d`/`Transform3f`, the clashing `using Domain::...` lines are gone, the optimizer namespace alias uses `namespace` instead of `using`, and `rotation_angles_to_transform` moved out of the anonymous namespace it was declared public from. The config-dependent `find_least_supports_rotation` stays removed. `sla_rotfinder_tests.cpp` (rewritten on real APIs) shows a 10x10x40 mm box laid down to under 11 mm.
    Follow-up 2026-09-29: the todo also asked for the best-misalignment rotation, which is already there, and for a least-supports mode. `SLAAutoOrient.hpp` gained `AutoOrientGoal{MinHeight, LeastSupports}` and `auto_orient(object, goal, status)`; `auto_orient_min_height` stays as a shorthand. LeastSupports uses Rotfinder's `find_least_supports_rotation`, re-added here as upstream's *on-floor* branch only, because that score (`get_supportedness_onfloor_score`) needs no print config while the other branch's `is_on_floor()` decision did; it scores the already-present `get_chull_rotations()` poses (a convex hull face laid flat), reusing the already-present `find_ground_level()`. Note `find_best_misalignment_rotation()` is not a supports objective (it *maximises* misalignment, so it tilts a box diagonally). The Rotate panel now offers "Lowest height" / "Fewest supports" next to the button, default fewest, remembered for the session. Tests: a 30°-tilted 40x10x10 mm box comes back on its largest face, and an object with no geometry returns no rotation instead of reading past the end of the instance list. Built and tested in build 32 (2026-09-30), both suites green; still not tried in the app.
  - [x] **M4.11b** The two objectives PLAN B7 still asks for and M4.11a did not have: least peel area (lay the object down so the biggest cross section on its way up is the smallest, the peak peel force of the print) and cup avoidance (lay it down so no layer of it is an upside-down cup), both estimated cheaply from the mesh, and both offered in the Rotate panel next to the existing goals. · M · needs M4.11a, M4.8e
    Result: the new `SLA/OrientCrossSection.{hpp,cpp}` scores one candidate pose by slicing the rotated mesh coarsely: planes 1 mm apart between the lowest and the highest vertex of the rotated mesh (between the extremes, because the slicer does not cut a horizontal face that lies exactly on a plane, and never more than one plane per 64 mm of height, so a tall pose costs no more than a short one). Two terms come out in mm²: the biggest of the slices, which is the peak peel area of `SLA/LayerStats.hpp`, and the openings of the suction cups `SLA/CavityDetection.hpp` finds on the same slices, which is the M4.8e rule run on the pose instead of on a print (a pocket enclosed in its layer that stays enclosed going up until a layer closes it), so the two goals agree with what the post-slice detection reports. `Rotfinder` drives both over the candidates the least-supports goal already uses (a convex hull face laid flat, the poses an object rests on the plate in), capped to the 24 biggest ones because slicing costs orders of magnitude more than the triangle walks of the other goals, and `find_least_peel_rotation` / `find_no_cups_rotation` sit next to the existing entries. The weights are in the header: LeastPeel is the peak area alone, NoCups is the cup openings plus 0.05 of the peak area, so a cup is only traded away for a big enough saving in peak area (5 mm² of peak area per 1 mm² of cup opening) and among the cup-free poses the one that peels easiest is still the one that is picked. Neither goal reads a print config or slices, which is what M4.11's result thought was missing. Rotfinder's searches now take a `TriangleMesh` and the `ModelObject` versions are thin wrappers over `mesh_to_rotate()`, so a caller holding no model can run them: `auto_orient_mesh(object)` takes the mesh out of the model on the UI thread and `auto_orient(mesh, goal, status)` runs the same search on it, with the progress and the cancel as before. The Rotate panel offers "Least peel" and "No cups" next to the two existing goals (the row wraps to a second line), and the search runs as a job manager job named `sla_auto_orient <project>`, which is what M4.12's result still lacks: the mesh is copied out of the model before the worker starts, the worker reports into the progress tracker, the button becomes "Cancel" while it runs and the engine is asked between the poses it scores, the rotation is applied on the main thread only if the element it was searched for is still the one selected, the one Rotate undo step is unchanged, and a pop notification (registered under `sla_auto_orient`) shows the progress when the panel is closed. Tests in `tests/sla_print/sla_rotfinder_tests.cpp`: a 60 x 40 x 2 mm plate comes back on its 40 x 2 mm edge by LeastPeel (80 mm² peak against the 2400 mm² that MinHeight lays it flat with, and much taller), an upside-down 20 mm cup (a floor and four walls around a pocket, opening on the vat) comes back with no cup and its opening off the plate, while the same cup opening up is cup free already and stays cup free, the mesh overload finds the same rotations as the model overload, a callback that refuses at once stops the search and still answers with a pose, and every search is run twice to show the answer does not depend on the order the poses came out of the parallel loop in. The coarse slices and the cups are measured in the test by slicing the mesh itself, so the engine is checked against something other than its own code. NOT BUILT: a human build and run is needed before this can be called green. Fix 2026-10-01 (found by a test build): the two goals sliced the mesh as if its vertices were millimetres, but `mesh_to_rotate()` hands them out in the scaled coordinates a model mesh is kept in, so a pose asked the slicer for a million planes per millimetre of height and a 256 mm² cup opening measured as 2.56e-10 mm², under the 1 mm² threshold of M4.8e, so the no cups goal could not see a cup at all. `score_cross_sections()` now scales the step of the planes with the Z it cuts at (and the layer thicknesses it hands the detection are in mm again), and its header no longer claims the mesh is in mm. The tests follow it: `MeshModel` builds its mesh in the coordinates an importer hands over, the measurement slices a millimetre apart in them, and the upside down cup case asks for the direction the pocket faces, which is the local -Z of the cup after the turn and not its +Z, the floor side, which would have failed as soon as the measurement found a cup.

- [ ] **M4.14** Peak memory when slicing for 12K and 16K displays (Photon Mono M5: 11520 × 5120, about 59 megapixels per layer), on an otherwise idle 8 GB machine. Record peak working set per step for a small and a tall model, and find which step holds full-resolution layer buffers. Evidence: 2026-09-22 a slice on the M5 profile ended in `std::bad_alloc`, with steps taking 3–6 minutes; a compiler build was running at the same time, so this is not yet known to be a real limit. · M · needs —
  Progress 2026-09-30: findings in `doc/sla-fork/profiling/raster-memory.md`; not ticked, the peak working set still has to be measured on a machine. The raw raster is one byte per pixel (`AGGRaster::m_buf` is a `std::vector<agg::pixfmt_gray8::pixel_type>`), so 59 MB per 12K layer. Only `slapsRasterize` ever holds them, one per layer in flight, and it ran every layer through a single unbounded `tbb::parallel_for` (granularity 1, no concurrency cap, unlike the merge step above it), so the peak was `min(cores, layers) x 59 MB` - 944 MB on 16 threads. `rasterize()` now walks the print in windows of `sla::raw_raster_batch_size()` (512 MB budget = 9 layers at 12K, clamped to the layer count and to the parallelism, so small displays and machines with 9 or fewer threads are unchanged), each rasterizer reports its own cost through the new `ISlaRasterizer::raw_raster_bytes()` (SVG reports 0), and `sla::live_raw_rasters()`/`peak_live_raw_rasters()` count the buffers. The 2026-09-22 `std::bad_alloc` was a different bug, the encoders' 59 MB per layer `reserve`, already fixed in `574213b5e1`. Also: `store_anycubic` collected the whole encoded print into a second buffer to write the layer definitions, now 36 bytes per layer instead; `store_goo` reserved 32 kB per layer and never used it, removed. Still open: the merge step keeps three copies of every layer's polygons (`m_printer_input` plus `all_layer_polygons` and `slices`), which is the larger term for a tall model, and nobody has measured the step times on 12K yet. Tests: 5 cases in `tests/sla_print/sla_raster_memory_tests.cpp` (budget arithmetic, clamps, and that a windowed loop caps the live rasters) and `src/slic3r-shared/test/Slic3r/Biz/ResultExport/SLA/SlaRasterMemoryTests.cpp`, which slices and exports a 1 mm cube at 11520 x 5120 and asserts the peak stayed inside the batch and inside the budget. Unbuilt.

  Result: review in `doc/sla-fork/profiling/aa-and-z-correction.md`: one rasterizer for all four formats, keyed on `gamma_correction` (0 = agg threshold = binary, > 0 = 8-bit AA); the encoders then quantize differently (sl1 keeps 8-bit greyscale PNG with no AA key in `config.ini`, Anycubic/pw0 and pm5 keep 16 levels, `.goo` keeps 16). On the Z side: `absolute_correction`, `elefant_foot_compensation` (ramped over `faded_layers`), then `zcorrection_layers` deleting cross-layer-bleed features from the model slices only. Three findings, none changed: the `.pwmo/.pwmx/.pwms` `antialiasing` flag and the `.goo` `anti_aliasing_level = 1` / `grey_level = 4` do not follow `gamma_correction` and understate what the encoders write, and neither can be settled without a sample file from a real printer; and the guard at the top of `apply_printer_corrections` (upstream's) skips the whole support origin, so the raft and the pillars never get a printer correction either. pm5 is correct (`PM5_LAYER_COLOR_LEVELS = 16`). New tests: `SlaAntiAliasingTests.cpp` (export with AA off and on to all four formats, check the header field and that the decoded pixels are binary or greyscale as gamma says) and `tests/sla_print/sla_layer_hash_tests.cpp` (FNV over the layer polygons, as in the benchmark harness: unchanged by `exposure_time` and `gamma_correction`, changed by `elefant_foot_compensation` and `absolute_correction`). Not built or run in this session.  Merge 2026-09-30: the shared `SlaLayerDecoders.hpp` is the M5.4 one (ctb, goo, pw0, png, svg), with `distinct_greys` and `on_quantization_grid` added for the two level checks the AA test makes. The `.ctb` writer (M5.3.ctb) has an anti-aliasing flag too and it is written as 1 whatever `gamma_correction` is, so `.ctb` joined the format list in `SlaAntiAliasingTests.cpp` and is a fourth instance of the first finding; the review doc predates the writer and does not list it. Still not built or run.
  - [x] **M4.13b** The anti-aliasing header fields follow `gamma_correction`, so a binary raster (gamma 0) no longer claims anti-aliasing. No printer sample exists for any of the containers involved, so nothing an AA-on export writes may change. · M · needs M4.13
    Result: checked first that AA is on by default: `gamma_correction` defaults to 1 (`ConfigDefsSLA.cpp`), none of the six community-sla printer presets sets it, and neither does the bundle's sla_print, so every stock profile rasterizes anti-aliased (the one research bundle that sets it, Photon Mono, sets 1). One answer for the writers, `sla_raster_anti_aliased()` in the new `Biz/ResultExport/SLA/SlaAntiAliasing.hpp`: `store_anycubic` writes the .pwmx `antialiasing` flag as 1 or 0, `store_goo` writes `anti_aliasing_level` 1/0 and `grey_level` 4 bits/1 bit, `store_ctb` writes word 29 as 1/0. An anti-aliased export is byte for byte what it was, since the AA value of each field is the constant the writer always wrote, so the existing export tests still hold. `.pm5` is deliberately left alone: its field counts the levels of the pw0 encoding (16, next to a 16-byte colour table), not the levels of the raster, which is the review's "not a finding: pm5". Still unverified, as before: no .pwmx, .goo or .ctb sample, so whether the .goo `grey_level` counts bits (the AA value 4 assumes so) or levels, and whether a firmware re-anti-aliases a binary file, are both open; the header holds the two values per field and the review doc's findings 1 and 2 record the change and the alternative reading. Tests: `SlaAntiAliasingTests.cpp` - the AA-off case now asserts the no-AA header value per format (flag 0, AA 0 with 1 grey bit, ctb 0) and the AA-on case stays pinned to today's values; the pm5 case is unchanged and now says why it does not move. Not built or run in this session.

## M5: Formats and inspection

- [x] **M5.1** Import `.sl1`/`.sl1s`/`.slx` archives in the new app, porting the legacy `SLAImportJob` (PLAN C1). · L → split · needs M0.10

Upstream had removed the reader, so this was a restore, not a port. Two things M5.1's own text does not ask for are still missing: the archive's print settings are read but not applied to the presets, and there is no progress bar, because the shared import path is synchronous for every format.
  Split 2026-09-22: upstream removed SLA import outright in `d9e89cf564` ("Remove SLA import", 901 lines over 11 files), so there is no reader to wire up; it has to be restored first.
  - [x] **M5.1a** Restore the SL1/SL1S archive reader into libslic3r from `d9e89cf564^` (`SLAArchiveReader`, `ZipperArchiveImport`, the reading half of `SL1`), plus the deleted round-trip test. Engine only, no UI. Do not restore the engine-level `SLAArchiveFormatRegistry`: format registration now lives in `Biz/ResultExport/SLA/SlaArchiveFormat` (M0.9). · M · needs M0.10
    Result (2026-09-29): restored as the split the Blocked note asked for. M5.1a1: `sla::layers_to_mesh` in the public `libslic3r/SLALayersToMesh.hpp`. M5.1a2: `import_sl1_archive` in `Biz/ResultExport/SLA/SL1Import` opens the zip with MiniZWrapper, parses `config.ini`/`prusaslicer.ini` with a plain key=value reader (no `DynamicPrintConfig`), decodes the layer pngs with `png::decode_png` and calls the engine function; a round-trip test slices a 20 mm cube, exports it with `store_sl1` and reads it back, plus the error cases. Wiring into the UI is M5.1b. Both halves are ticked below.
  - [x] **M5.1a1** Engine: one public function that turns decoded layer images into a mesh. · M · needs M0.10
    Result: `sla::layers_to_mesh()` in `libslic3r/SLALayersToMesh.hpp` takes decoded grayscale layers plus pixel size, display size, mirroring and layer heights and returns a `Domain::TriangleMesh`: marching squares per layer, the raster trafo inverted so a rendered layer comes back in place, then the outlines stacked with `slices_to_mesh()`. Only public Domain headers are used in the header, so Biz can import archives without reaching libslic3r's private ones. Tested: both suites green 2026-09-30.
  - [x] **M5.1a2** Biz: open the archive, parse its ini files and decode its layer images. · M · needs M5.1a1
    Result: `import_sl1_archive()` in `Biz/ResultExport/SLA/SL1Import` opens a `.sl1`/`.sl1s` with `MiniZWrapper`, reads `config.ini` and `prusaslicer.ini` with a plain key=value parser, sorts the layer images by name for the layer order and decodes each to 8-bit gray with `png::decode_png` (gray first, rgba collapsed to luminance as a fallback). Display size, pixel counts, orientation, mirroring and layer heights come from the profile keys, with `config.ini`'s `layerHeight` as the layer-height fallback; missing metadata, an archive without layer images and an undecodable layer are errors. A round-trip test slices a 20 mm cube off both display centre lines, exports it with `store_sl1` and reads it back, plus hand-built error cases. Tested: both suites green 2026-09-30.
  - [x] **M5.1b** Wire the reader into `FileLoadingLogic` and `get_import_extensions()`, as a job with progress and cancel. · M · needs M5.1a
    Result (2026-09-29): `.sl1` and `.sl1s` are in `get_import_extensions()`, so the File > Import dialog, the "All supported files" pattern and the drag and drop filter all take them; `read_data_from_file()` calls `import_sl1_archive` and hands the mesh to the shared path, which adds it as a model object named after the file, and the reader's message goes out through the usual "Files import" error dialog. The archive's print settings are read but not applied to the presets (later step). No job: the shared import path (`ProjectInteractor::load_models_to_project` from the File menu and from the drop target) is synchronous for every format, so there is no progress bar or stop flag to pass. The millimetre scale of the reconstructed mesh comes from the profile, so the meters/inches guessing of the mesh formats is skipped for archives. No test: `FileLoadingLogic` has no test file, so the extension list is not covered.
  - [x] **M5.1c** The archive's print settings are offered after the import, and the archive import itself runs as a job with progress and cancel. · M · needs M5.1b
    Result: this is the work M5.1's own result note lists as still missing (the settings are read but not applied, and there is no progress bar); the note above is left as it was. **Settings.** `SlaArchiveSettingsNotification` (App/Plater) listens to `on_instance_added`, finds the archive the new object came from in its `ModelVolume::Source::input_file`, and runs the existing resin import as a **dry run** through `ResinProfileImportInteractor`. If the mapping writes at least one resin value it shows a notification with one button, "Use the settings from this archive", which opens the M3.10 review on that archive; the user reads what each value becomes there and saves them as a user resin preset. Nothing is written and the selected printer is never touched, so the offer is the only thing that happens unasked. Two gaps had to be closed for the review to be usable at all: `default_registry()` registered only `ChituboxCfgReader`, so an `.sl1` was not readable by the dialog or by `--import-resin-profile` at all (it now calls `register_resin_profile_readers()`), and the mapping table had none of the archive's own spellings — `expTime`, `expTimeFirst` and `numFade` are now sources next to the Chitubox ones (`layerHeight` was already there). All three are same-unit copies, `store_sl1()` writes them straight off the config keys. The archive's bookkeeping (`jobDir`, `action`, `printTime`, `usedMaterial`, `numSlow`, `numFast`, `hollow`, ...) is now one reported rule instead of a dozen Unknown rows. The supports, raft and hollowing of the archive's prusaslicer.ini have no resin mapping, so `App/Plater/SlaArchiveSettings` names them in the notification ("Not applied, these are print settings: ...", config keys, capped at 6 with the rest counted) rather than dropping them in silence. **Progress and cancel.** `read_sla_archives()` in `Biz/FileLoadingLogic` is the job body with the threads taken out (`import_sla_archives_local()` is the JobManager adapter over it); `import_sl1_archive()` and `sla::layers_to_mesh()` grew an optional progress callback, decoding the layer images being two thirds of the bar and tracing them into a mesh the rest. `ProjectInteractor::load_models_to_project()` splits the archives off and starts the job `sla_archive_import`; every other format keeps the synchronous path it had. The mesh goes on the plate, is arranged and gets its undo step in `on_result`, back on the main thread, and a cancelled job returns nothing so nothing reaches the project. `JobNotificationSpec` grew a `cancelable` flag and a new `PopNotificationLayoutHeaderProgressButtons` layout puts a Cancel button on the running job's bar, which calls `request_job_stop`; the existing arrange, simplify and repair notifications are unchanged. **Tests.** `SlaArchiveImportJobTests.cpp` drives the job body with a fake reader: the progress is monotonic from 0 to 1, a cancel asked for before, during or between archives adds nothing, a broken archive among good ones is reported without stopping the rest, and `is_sla_archive_file()` decides what counts. `SlaArchiveSettingsTests.cpp` checks that the archive's spellings reach the resin keys, that the notification is built with the action for an archive with settings and is not built at all for one without, that the button opens the review, and that the not-applied names and their cap are right. Not built or run in this session; the human builds and runs the suites. Fix 2026-10-01 (found by a test build): the case asked for `material_density` among the applied values, but the label table of `analyze_archive_settings()` calls that value "Density", so the list holds labels and not resin keys; the value of that archive the table has no label for is the bottle size, so the case asks for `bottle_volume`. No product change.

Each pair of layers becomes a binary mask (grey 8 of 16 or more is lit), the two are lined up on their centroids and the IoU is reported under identity, mirror X, mirror Y and 180 degrees, with identity required to be the best and the winning transform named when it is not. Method written up in `doc/sla-fork/formats/orientation.md`, without numbers. Left: nobody has run it, so `.pm5` mirroring is still unconfirmed, and a low IoU under all four transforms would mean the two slicers produced different cross sections rather than that a mirror is wrong.

  Progress 2026-09-30: the removable-drive half is done and tested. The destination already went through the shared path (`ExportActions::make_export` → `show_export_modal_dialog` → `ProjectInteractor::do_result_export`), so the extension came from `SlaExportFileTypes`, the M5.9 unfinished-slice check and the error reporting applied, and `PrintHostLocal` reported the target, which is what puts Eject on the finished-export notification; there was no FFF-only or SLA-only gate in any of it. The one thing that blocked it: `PhysicalPrinterInteractor::is_printer_compatible` accepted only the hard-coded default entry (Local Drive) for a filesystem destination, so Removable Drive came back "incompatible" and `on_selected_config_container_changed()` reset the destination to the local one as soon as the printer or the plate changed, putting the file on the local disk instead of the stick. A filesystem destination never talks to a printer, so it is now compatible with every profile, FFF and SLA alike. Left: nothing uploads to a print host yet, and no test covers the destination logic (there is no fixture for `PhysicalPrinterInteractor`). Both suites green 2026-09-30.

Survey first which of the print hosts can take a resin file at all: the SL1 runs PrusaLink's firmware and speaks its REST API, so PrusaLink and Prusa Connect take `.sl1`/`.sl1s`, and the rest (OctoPrint, Moonraker, Duet, FlashAir, AstroBox, Repetier, MKS) are FFF servers that take G-Code. A host that cannot read the printer's format has to say so rather than upload. · M · needs —
    Result: the rule is one pure Biz function, `PrintHostFormats` (`print_host_accepts_extension`, `destination_accepts_extension`, `destination_rejects_extension_message`), with 5 Catch2 cases over every host type, a filesystem destination and Prusa Connect; no network. The upload path needed no new plumbing: `ExportActions::upload_gcode_to_print_host` -> `show_upload_modal_dialog` -> `do_result_upload` already ended in the same `do_result_export_inner` as the file export, so the extension came from `SlaExportFileTypes`, the M5.9 unfinished-slice check and the M5.9a failure notification applied, and the finalizer writes the archive with the format registry. Two things were missing. The upload dialog took a typed name without asking whether it fits the plate's format, so it now says what the export dialog says and offers to choose again (`warn_sla_format_mismatch`), and its title is "Send print file to printer host" on an SLA bed instead of "Send G-Code to printer host". And `do_result_upload` / `do_result_upload_connect` now refuse a file the destination cannot read — "OctoPrint does not accept .pm5 files" — through the same failure notification, so no file is uploaded that the host would only store. Not tested in the app; tests are not run here (a human builds).

What is covered: the list itself (Local Drive, Removable Drive and Prusa Connect in that order, the local one selected without being asked for, the two drives being the same kind of entry told apart by `FileSystemExport::prefer_removable`); `can_be_selected`, where everything but Connect needs no account and a test project is not logged in; `select_connect_upload`'s prefer-a-picked-printer rule; `is_printer_compatible` over both drives, Connect and a stored host upload against the FFF, SLA and other-FFF hardware configs - the M5.5 rule that a filesystem destination fits every profile is pinned there, and so is a host upload being bound to the hardware config it was added with; the per-plate memory, where a picked removable drive survives adding a plate and each plate restores its own destination; and the reset itself, where a host upload picked for an FFF plate is dropped for a plate added on the Photon Mono M5 and the destination falls back to the local drive instead of uploading a `.pm5` to an FFF print host. Reuse the clipping plane the paint and SLA tools already use (`Scene::Clipper`, `Scene::ClipperPresenter`) rather than adding a second mechanism. · M · needs M1.3
  Result: the clipper holds one plane, so the band's lower limit became it (`Clipper::set_height_band`, caps come from the existing `fill_cut`) and the upper limit a new `clipping_plane_2` uniform in `gouraud_light_clip` (110/140/ES; unset it clips nothing). `Plater::SlaHeightBand` - a left-column panel, SLA-only, off by default, 0..`max_print_height`, reset button, suspended while a tool gizmo owns the shared clipper. `HeightBand` math unit-tested in `HeightBandTests.cpp`. Scope gap: the presenter's subtree replaces the scene nodes it does not draw, so only the selected model object is capped; capped supports and pad need the meshes fed to the clipper and are left for a follow-up (M5.10b). Built and tested in build 32 (2026-09-30), both suites green; not checked in the app. Build 32 also found that both limits were inverted: `height_band_upper_plane_data()` returned `(0, 0, 1, -z_max)` and `Clipper::set_height_band` passed the lower normal un-negated, while the shaders discard when `dot(world_position, clipping_plane) < 0` and the clipper keeps `n.p <= offset` - so the band hid everything instead of showing it. Both are `-UnitZ` now.

The band no longer needs a selection, and follows the bed selection and the cache. Built and tested in build 32 (2026-09-30), both suites green; not checked in the app.

## M6: Quality gates and release

- [x] **M6.1** Robustness mesh set with no crashes or hangs (PLAN G2). · M · needs M0.13
  Progress: `tests/sla_print/sla_robustness_tests.cpp` builds 11 bad meshes in code (empty, single triangle, degenerate triangle, open box, box with a flipped face, two overlapping cubes in one volume, cube with duplicated faces, 0.05 mm cube, 0.02 mm plate, 0.5 × 0.5 × 150 mm needle, cube 1500 mm from the origin) and runs each through support point generation, the support tree and raft, and a full slice. Every support call gets a 60 s stop function; the far-from-origin case runs both stages on a worker thread with a deadline, because the support generator has run for minutes on a mesh at ~1364 mm. Not built: the test target was not compiled, so nothing is known about what the pipeline does with these meshes yet. Left unticked until it passes and until its `needs` (M0.13) is done.
    Progress 2026-09-30: the test is compiled and runs in `sla_print_tests` (108/108 pass), so the 11 bad meshes go through support point generation, the support tree and raft and a full slice without a crash or a hang, and the 60 s stop function and the worker-thread deadline on the far-from-origin case do their job. Build 32 also hardened the two reads this set ran into: `SLAPrint::Steps::support_points` no longer reads `m_model_height_levels[1]` when the object is one layer tall or thinner (the assert that covered it is compiled out in Release; it falls back to `Domain::sla_effective_layer_height()` with a warning), `initialize_printer_input()` checks the slice index before taking `m_slice_index.front()` and no longer assigns a bool to the layer count, and `apply_printer_corrections()` tests the `m_supportable_mesh` optional. Still unticked: the todo's `needs` (M0.13, the benchmark harness) is not done, so a fixture-only pass over synthetic meshes is not yet the robustness run the todo asks for.
  Result: the set is generated in code (no files from tests/data) and now holds 20 bad meshes: empty, single triangle, zero area triangle, open box, box with a square hole in a face, box with one flipped face, box with every normal inverted, box with three faces on one edge (a non-manifold edge), cube with its faces twice, box with one triangle twice at the same coordinates, two overlapping cubes in one volume, two crossing shells, two cubes touching at a face, 0.05 mm cube, 0.02 mm plate, 0.1 x 0.1 x 150 mm needle, a one metre cube, a cube 100 km from the origin, a cube floating 50 mm above the plate with supports off, and a box with a NaN and an infinity among its vertices. Every case runs the support tool (points, then tree and raft) and a full SLAPrint slice on a worker thread under a watchdog: `std::async` plus `wait_for`, and a stage that overruns its budget is asked to stop (the support tool through its stop function, the slice through `IPrint::stop_token`, the same handle BackgroundProcess uses) and gets 30 s to unwind; a stage that neither finishes nor takes the stop fails the test instead of hanging the binary. Only a `Slic3r::RuntimeError` or a `Slic3r::Biz::Slicing::Exception` carrying a message counts as a handled refusal, anything else escaping a stage is recorded and fails the case. Two guards went in with it: `SLAPrint::slice()` refuses a model whose mesh has a vertex that is not a number before the first step reads it (the bounding box helpers assert on such a box and `scaled()` of it is not a level, so the slice index would run across the whole coordinate range), and `compute_slice_heights()` in the support tool returns no heights for a box that is not a range of coordinates or a layer height that is not a step, which is the loop that never ends on them. Not built and not run here (a human builds and runs both suites); nothing was checked in the app. M0.13 stays open and is the separate benchmark run this set feeds — the set itself does not need it.

The same rule is a unit test in `FixtureRenderTests.cpp`, so a palette change cannot collapse the model, the supports and the pad unnoticed; the python tool is run over fixtures made for it. `doc/sla-fork/visual-regression.md` has the commands and how to make a reference. `visual_diff.py` and its lightness math were run here; the app was not built, so the render path and the new tests are not.
  Left: no references and no probe files are committed, so nothing has been run end to end - the first render is a person's job and is what will show whether the offscreen path needs a fix. - [x] **M6.3** Nightly upstream merge rehearsal with a conflict report (PLAN G4). · S · needs M0.3, M0.14
  Result: `doc/sla-fork/tools/upstream_rehearsal.py` (standard library only) is the whole rehearsal, and it is local first. It fetches the read-only `upstream` remote, counts the upstream commits since the last merge base, then creates a throwaway worktree under the gitignored `.agent-scratch/`, detached at the branch tip, runs `git merge --no-commit --no-ff` there, counts the conflict hunks of every unmerged file and writes a Markdown report (and `--json` for CI), and aborts the merge and removes the worktree in a `finally`. No branch can move, because a merge in a detached worktree cannot update one; nothing is ever pushed; the tip is read again at the end and the report says whether it moved. Conflicted files are grouped as engine SLA / config defs / App/Biz / build / other, each with its hunk count and whether it is a PLAN 3.3 hotspot file, and the hotspots get their own section, because those are merged ahead of other work in small PRs. Exit code 0 clean, 1 conflicts, 2 the run itself failed. `.github/workflows/upstream_rehearsal.yml` is `workflow_dispatch` only and is its own small file, since M0.14 has no workflow file yet: a full clone (a shallow one has no merge base), the upstream remote added by the job, the report in the job summary and uploaded as an artifact, and a conflict fails the job only when the input says so. The commands, the remotes and the exit codes are in [ci.md](ci.md). `doc/sla-fork/tools/test_upstream_rehearsal.py` tests the report builder as a pure function on a made up conflict list (22 unittest cases: area grouping, the hotspot list, hunk counts, the Markdown, the JSON, the exit code). Not run yet: no merge was rehearsed here, so the first real run, on a machine with the upstream remote, is a person's job.
  Left: PLAN G4 also asks for a build and a test run of the merged tree. That is not in the script (it would need the whole build, which is what the M0.14 runner is for) and is not done; the workflow is manual rather than nightly, so the schedule line in ci.md is the follow-up once M0.14 lands; and the todo's `needs` M0.14 is still open.
  - [x] **M6.3b** Merge the two `doc/sla-fork/ci.md` texts into one document. Two jobs wrote the file on parallel branches (M0.14 and M6.3) and the merge stacked them, so it ended up with two H1s and repeated sections. · S · needs M6.3, M0.14
    Result: docs only, no workflow and no code changed, and nothing was run. `doc/sla-fork/ci.md` is now one document with one H1: an overview (both workflows are manual only, no `push` and no `schedule`, neither has ever been executed on a runner), `sla-ci.yml` (what a run does, the by-hand and `run-ci` label triggers, the cost, the timeouts, the `layer_hash` gate and the six steps that were never verified), the upstream rehearsal (local use of `upstream_rehearsal.py`, what one run does, the report, the exit codes, the report builder tests and the manual workflow), and a short note that the upstream workflows in `.github/workflows` (`build_windows`, `build_osx`, `build_flatpak` and `clang_format` on every push, `static_analysis` nightly) stay untouched for mergeability, so Actions on this fork should be disabled or restricted to the two workflows. Every fact of both texts is kept; what went is the duplication and two sentences the merge had made stale (the rehearsal is no longer described as waiting for an M0.14 workflow file, and `Not covered` no longer says the rehearsal has no job).

- [x] **M6.2d** Nothing runs the tool in CI yet: add a job beside M0.14's that renders the fixtures, runs `visual_diff.py` against the committed references and fails on a changed image or on a palette lightness the PLAN 2.1 rule rejects. · M · needs M6.2, M0.14
  Result: a `visual-regression` job in `.github/workflows/sla-ci.yml` beside `build-and-test`, with the same manual gate (workflow_dispatch, or the `run-ci` label on a pull request) and its own runner, sharing the deps cache key. It starts cheap: `test_visual_ci.py`, then `doc/sla-fork/tools/visual_ci.py plan`, which reads `doc/sla-fork/visual-regression/manifest.json` (id, fixture, view, size, reference, probes - the layout is in visual-regression.md, written by whoever makes the first references) and writes `have_references`; every step that costs minutes is skipped while that is false, so with no committed references the job is green and says why instead of building an app to compare against nothing. Otherwise it builds `slic3r-app-launcher` (the binary that takes `--render-to`), renders every entry under `xvfb-run` with llvmpipe - the render is offscreen but the GL context belongs to the window - and runs `visual_diff.py check` on it, which fails the job on a changed image or on a lightness pair the PLAN 2.1 rule rejects. `visual_ci.py` is the glue, standard library only: the manifest, the two command lines, the loop, the Markdown report and the exit codes (0 passed or nothing to compare, 1 a render changed, 2 the run could not be made) are pure functions, so `test_visual_ci.py` covers them in 48 unit tests with a fake app and a fake `visual_diff.py` and needs no build; the loop was also run end to end here against a fake app writing real PNGs and a real sidecar (a pass, a changed picture and a 2 on a refused render). Neither the tool nor the job has been run against a real app or on a runner, and the render path itself is still unbuilt (M6.2a, M6.2b and M6.2c are open). `ci.md` and visual-regression.md carry the cost, the exit codes, the manifest layout and the first-run watch list: headless GL, a dialog that could block a render, and whether a Linux render matches a Windows reference at all.
- [x] **M6.2b** `--render-view preview` draws the sliced print the way the objects list thumbnails do, not the Preview tab with its own camera, so a change in that view is invisible to the check: render the Preview view itself. · M · needs M6.2
    Result: `--render-view preview` renders the Preview tab itself. The new `App::IFixtureViewSource`
    (App/FixtureRender.hpp) is what a render needs from a view, and `PreviewScenePresenter` implements it: its own
    scene, its own `PreviewSceneRenderCustomizer` and its camera as the `CameraSynchData` the two views hand
    each other on a tab switch (`current_camera_synch_data()`, which `on_deactivated` uses now too). The app
    hands the source to `ThumbnailImageGenerator::set_fixture_view_source`, the render goes through
    `generate_thumbnails` with the view's customizer and a camera built by `set_fixture_view_camera()` - the
    same `synchronize_camera` the modules use, so the eye, the target, the angles and the zoom of the view are
    what a change in the view has to show up in, and the viewport of the size asked for is the only thing a
    render changes. The gcode-thumbnail path is out of the render: a preview render draws the Preview scene and
    not the plater one. The sidecar grew a `scene` key ("plater" / "preview") and a view with no print in its
    scene is not rendered: `fixture_render_has_print()` skips the bed, and `do_fixture_render` asks a view again
    instead of writing a reference of an empty bed. 91 lines of tests in `FixtureRenderTests.cpp`: the view to
    scene mapping, the camera handover (zoom, projection, the eye at the distance of the target, the forward
    axis, a second view giving a second camera, an orthographic view rendered orthographic, the viewport left
    to the renderer) and the sidecar key. The render path itself is GL and has no unit test; nothing was built
    or run here, and M6.2a is still the first end to end run. The M6.2 note above still says the preview draws
    the objects list thumbnail: that line is left as it was written.

## M7: Excellent auto-supports

> **Parked (2026-09-18).** The priority is the regular support function: the support points tool (M2.1–M2.3) and support generation quality (M4.3–M4.5). M7.4 work in progress is on the unmerged branch `sla/M7.4-rule-mining`. Known issues there: tip width saturates at the search radius, and registration error (0.5–0.9 mm RMS) exceeds the contact gap, so most contacts are missed. Refine registration on the model-only vertices before detecting contacts.

**Goal:** on typical models (miniatures, busts, functional parts), auto-generated supports print reliably and match what an experienced person would do. Someone experienced shouldn't *need* to hand-edit them, and cleanup should leave no scars on detailed or cosmetic surfaces.

**Approach**
1. **Interview:** write down expert knowledge as explicit rules (M7.1).
2. **Research dataset:** supported scenes made by an experienced person in another slicer (currently Lychee), exported as plain meshes by that slicer (M7.2).
3. **Offline analysis:** scripts in `tools/support-research/` (Python, not part of the app build) extract every support contact (M7.3) and mine measurable rules from them (M7.4).
4. **Rulebook:** interview rules and mined rules are merged and reviewed (M7.5).
5. **Scoring:** the generator is scored against the expert supports, with a baseline first (M7.6, M7.7).
6. **Implementation:** the generator is improved rule by rule, with every change scored, then tuned (M7.8–M7.11).
7. **Confirmation:** real prints check the result (M7.12).

**Research-only rules**
- **Nothing is ported.** The dataset is only for *learning rules*. PrusaSLA gets no reader for other slicers' project formats, and the data comes from the authoring slicer's own mesh export. Don't decode `.lys` or other protected project files.
- **Personal-use license.** The current dataset is Factory Fortress Trench Crusade miniatures. Their Terms of Use grant a personal-use, single-computer license; forbid sharing, distribution and commercial or employer use; and allow modification only for personal use. So:
  - **Files stay in place.** Leave them in the owner's own folder, referenced through a manifest. Don't copy them into the repo tree or into `local-samples/`.
  - **Files never leave this computer.** No CI, cloud agents, uploads or file sharing.
  - **Agents don't open mesh files** in their context. They run local analysis scripts and read only the aggregate output.
  - **Only aggregate numbers are committed**, such as spacing, angles and diameters, never geometry or per-model contact lists. The rulebook, and synthetic test shapes made from scratch, can be committed.
  - **It stays a personal research activity.** If this work is done for an employer, the owner must check the license first.

- [ ] **M7.3** Contact extraction script. · L → split · needs M7.2
  - **Register the pair.** A check on 3 pairs (2026-09-17) found the supported STLs are **not** in the unsupported model's frame: they are rotated and moved into print orientation, and sometimes remeshed (one head had 31k triangles supported vs 260k unsupported). No triangles matched even at 0.01 mm. So:
    - Estimate the rigid transform (rotation + translation, no scale) from the unsupported model into the supported file: coarse alignment (principal axes plus the ambiguous flips, or feature-based global registration), then point-to-plane ICP on the model surface only, ignoring the support region as outliers.
    - Report the residual (RMS and 95th-percentile surface distance) and the recovered print tilt. Reject pairs above tolerance.
  - **Separate supports from the model:** after registration, take faces of the supported mesh farther than ε from the transformed model surface. Triangle-exact matching must not be assumed, because the model may be remeshed.
  - **Record orientation:** the recovered rotation is itself data for the orientation rules (M7.4).
  - **Describe each contact:** position, surface normal, contact diameter, penetration estimate, height above the plate, local overhang angle and curvature, and whether it serves a new island or local minimum (found by slicing the model mesh into layers).
  - **Record structure:** base/raft type and trunk and branch counts.
  - **Outputs** (under `local-samples/supports/out/`, gitignored and never committed): `contacts.json`, plus a colored debug mesh for a quick check by eye.

  Done when: a synthetic test shape with procedurally placed supports, then rotated, moved and remeshed, gives back the known transform and every known contact within tolerance.

Done when: results identical to the exact method on the existing synthetic tests, plus a benchmark on a synthetic mesh of at least 300k faces showing time and peak memory. · M · needs M7.3b
  Result: `separate_supports_scaled()` in `tools/support-research/separate.py`. Nearest-sample distance bounds the true distance from above, so a face is model as soon as that bound is below epsilon (no margin), while the support side needs a safety margin derived from the achieved sample spacing; only the band between is measured exactly. Sample count follows surface area with a cap, and scene faces are processed in chunks. Benchmarks (synthetic, in memory): 348k faces 1.3 s / 70 MB peak, 1.39M faces 3.7 s / 279 MB peak (process RSS 354 MB), 100% agreement with the known split and 400/400 faces identical to the exact method. 50 separation tests pass in 9.8 s, including sparse-sampling and chunking equivalence.
